<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{t:2d_estimates}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution of $\alpha$-Ricci flow on the 2-torus $M=\mathbb{T}^2$ with initial metric $g(0)$ and closed 1-form $\alpha(0)$. Then 
    there exists a constant $C=C(g(0),\alpha(0))$ such that the following hold
    \begin{itemize}
        \item The flow exists on $[0,\infty)$;
        \item The volume $V(t)$ satisfies the bound: $V(0)\le V(t) \le V(0)\sqrt{1+\frac{Ct}{2}}$;
        \item The $\alpha$-norm satisfies $|\alpha(t)|_{g(t)}^2\le \frac{C}{1+Ct/2}$;
        \item Type III estimate: $|R(x,t)|\le \frac{C}{1+t}$;
        \item The systole growth lower bound:
        \begin{align*}
            L_\sigma(t)^2\ge \frac{[\alpha](\sigma)^2}{2}t+L_\sigma(0)^2,
        \end{align*}
        where $\sigma\in H_1(M,\mathbb{Z})$, $L_\sigma(t)=\inf\{L(\gamma,g(t)):\gamma\in \sigma\}$, and $[\alpha](\sigma):=\int_\sigma\alpha$;
        \item The diameter estimate: $\operatorname{diam}(M,g(t)) \le C(\sqrt{t}+1)$.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
Note that in the arguments, we use the Gauss--Bonnet theorem in an essential way. Therefore, the estimates are specific to the 2-torus.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{d:rationality}
     We say that $[\alpha]$ is \textit{rational} if $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})\cong \mathbb{Z}$, and $[\alpha]$ is \textit{irrational} if $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z}) = \{0\}$.
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{d:prmitive length}
     If $[\alpha]$ is rational, there exists a unique generator $v$ of $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})$ such that $[\alpha]=\ell v$ for a unique constant $\ell >0$. We call $\ell=:\ell([\alpha]) $ the \textit{primitive length} of $[\alpha]$. If $[\alpha]$ is irrational, we define the primitive length $\ell([\alpha])=0$ in view of the fact that $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z}) = \{0\}$.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{r:remark_on_the_rationality}
     When $[\alpha] = 0$, i.e. $\alpha$ is exact, we also define $\ell([\alpha])=0$. Note that we don't say that $[\alpha]$ is irrational in this case since Definition~\ref{d:rationality} requires $[\alpha]\neq0$ (cf. Remark~\ref{r:lott.sesum}).
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{r:connection.ricci.harmonic}
    If $[\alpha]$ is rational, then there is a map $h(t):M\to S^1$, such that $\alpha(t)=\ell([\alpha])\nabla h(t)=\ell([\alpha])h(t)^* d\theta$ where $d\theta$ is the volume form of $S^1$ under the standard metric. In this case, the $\alpha$-Ricci flow reduces to 
    \begin{align*}
        	\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}=&-2\operatorname{Ric}+c\nabla h\otimes\nabla h\\
	\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}=&\tau_{g(t)}h.
    \end{align*}
    where $c=\frac{1}{2}\ell([\alpha])^2$ which is the Ricci-Harmonic map flow introduced in \cite{muller2012ricci}.
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{d:pointed.cinfty.convergence}
Let $\{(M_i, g_i(t),\alpha_i(t), p_i)\}_{i=1}^\infty$ be a sequence of families of metrics and closed 1-forms defined on pointed manifolds $(M_i,p_i)$ for $t \in (0,\infty)$. We say that
\[
(M_i, g_i(t),\alpha_i(t), p_i) \xrightarrow{pt\ C^\infty} (M_\infty, g_\infty(t),\alpha_\infty(t), p_\infty)
\]
if there exist:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item An exhaustion $\{U_j\}_{j=1}^\infty$ of $M_\infty$ with $p_\infty \in U_j$;
    
    \item Open subsets $V_{i,j} \subset M_i$ with $p_i \in V_{i,j}$ and diffeomorphisms
    \[
    \phi_{i,j} : U_j \to V_{i,j}, \qquad \phi_{i,j}(p_\infty)=p_i;
    \]
    
    \item For all $j$,
    \[
    \phi_{i,j}^* g_i(t) \to g_\infty(t), \qquad \phi_{i,j}^* \alpha_i(t) \to \alpha_\infty(t)
    \]
    smoothly on $U_j \times [j^{-1},j]$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- definition-4: body_md -->
\label{d:equivariant.pointed.cinfty.convergence}
In addition to the above, suppose that $G_i$ acts isometrically on $(M_i,g_i(1))$ and preserves $\alpha_i(1)$. For $l>0$, write
\[
G_i(l):=\{\gamma\in G_i : d_{g_i(1)}(\gamma p_i,p_i)\le l\}.
\]

We say that
\[
(M_i, g_i(1),\alpha_i(1),G_i, p_i) \xrightarrow{pt\ eq\ C^\infty} (M_\infty, g_\infty(1),\alpha_\infty(1),G_\infty, p_\infty)
\]
if:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item $(M_i, g_i(t),\alpha_i(t), p_i) \xrightarrow{pt\ C^\infty} (M_\infty, g_\infty(t),\alpha_\infty(t), p_\infty)$ with maps $\phi_{i,j}$ as above;

    \item $G_\infty \subset \mathrm{Isom}(M_\infty,g_\infty(1))$ is a closed subgroup preserving $\alpha_\infty(1)$;

    \item For every $j$ and all sufficiently large $i$, there exist maps
    \[
    \varphi_{i,j} : G_i(j) \to G_\infty(j+1), \qquad \psi_{i,j} : G_\infty(j) \to G_i(j+1),
    \]
    such that:
    
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\alph*)]
        \item For every $\gamma \in G_i(j)$ and $x \in U_j$ with $\gamma(\phi_{i,j}(x)) \in V_{i,j}$,
        \[
        d_{g_\infty(1)}\big(\phi_{i,j}^{-1}(\gamma(\phi_{i,j}(x))), \varphi_{i,j}(\gamma)(x)\big) \to 0;
        \]
        
        \item For every $\eta \in G_\infty(j)$ and $x \in U_j$ with $\psi_{i,j}(\eta)(\phi_{i,j}(x)) \in V_{i,j}$,
        \[
        d_{g_\infty(1)}\big(\phi_{i,j}^{-1}(\psi_{i,j}(\eta)(\phi_{i,j}(x))), \eta(x)\big) \to 0;
        \]
    \end{enumerate}
    as $i \to \infty$, uniformly over the indicated group elements and points $x\in U_j$ satisfying the respective conditions.
\end{enumerate}

By \cite[Theorem 2-1]{fukaya1986theory}, if $(M_i, g_i(1),\alpha_i(1), p_i) \xrightarrow{pt\ eq\ C^\infty} (M_\infty, g_\infty(1),\alpha_\infty(1), p_\infty)$, then we have $(M_i/G_i, d_{g_i(1)})\to (M_\infty/G_\infty,d_{g_\infty(1)})$ in Gromov--Hausdorff topology with the following commutative diagram\begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
  & (M_i, g_i(1),\alpha_i(1), G_i, p_i) 
  \arrow[r, "pt\ eq\ C^\infty"] 
  \arrow[d, "\pi_{i}"]
   & (M_\infty, g_\infty(1),\alpha_\infty(1),G_\infty, p_\infty) 
   \arrow[d, "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (M_i/G_i, d_{g_i(1)})
  \arrow[r, "GH"]
   & (M_\infty/G_\infty,d_{g_\infty(1)}).
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
<!-- end definition-4 -->
<!-- head-1: body_md -->
To simplify our notation, we write $(M,g_s(1))\xrightarrow{GH} (X,d)$ if $(M,g_s(1))$ converges to $(X,d)$ in Gromov--Hausdorff sense as $s\to\infty$.
Moreover, we write $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ 
to denote the pointed Cheeger-Gromov convergence on the universal cover.
If we replace $s$ by $s_i$, then the above convergences are along the sequence $s_i\to\infty$.
<!-- end head-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{t:main.GH_limit}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{T}^2$ defined for $[0,\infty)$, and suppose that $[\alpha]\neq 0$. Then we have the following.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item If $[\alpha]$ is rational, then $(M,t^{-1}g(t))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d([\alpha]))$ as $t\to\infty$. On $\tilde M$, we have $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$, where $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ is as in \eqref{eq:flat_expander_model}. Moreover, we have the following equivariant convergence
        \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
  & (\tilde{M},\tilde{g}_{s}(1), \tilde\alpha_{s}(1), G_{s}, p) 
  \arrow[r, "pt\ eq\ C^\infty"] 
  \arrow[d, "\pi"]
       & (\mathbb{R}^2,g^*(1), \alpha^*(1), G_\infty, p_\infty) 
   \arrow[d, "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (M, g_{s}(1)) 
  \arrow[r, "GH"]
   & (S^1,d([\alpha]))
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
        where
        $G_s=\pi_1(M)\subset \operatorname{Isom}(\tilde g_s(1))$ and $G_\infty\cong \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}\subset \operatorname{Isom}( g^*(1))$ acts on $\mathbb{R}^2$ by translations generated by $\partial_v$ and $(\theta,v)\mapsto (\theta+\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt{2},v)$.
         \item If $[\alpha]$ is irrational, then there is a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that 
         $(M,s_i^{-1}g(s_i))\xrightarrow{GH}~\{\mathrm{pt}\}$ and 
         on the universal cover, $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.  Moreover, we have the following equivariant convergence
        \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
  & (\tilde{M},\tilde{g}_{s_i}(1), \tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1), G_{i}, p_i) 
  \arrow[r, "pt\ eq\ C^\infty"] 
  \arrow[d, "\pi"]
   & (\mathbb{R}^2,g^*(1), \alpha^*(1), G_\infty, p_\infty) 
   \arrow[d, "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (M, g_{s_i}(1)) 
  \arrow[r, "GH"]
   & \{\mathrm{pt}\}
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
        where $G_i=\pi_1(M)\subset \operatorname{Isom}(\tilde g_i(1))$ and $G_\infty\cong \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\subset \operatorname{Isom}( g^*(1))$ is the translation subgroup of the Euclidean isometry group.
     \end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
\label{r:lott.sesum}
    Lott and Sesum’s result \cite{lott2014ricci} treats the case $[\alpha]=0$, where $(M,g(t))$ converges to a flat $2$-torus and hence $(M,t^{-1}g(t))$ collapses to a point and $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$ as $s\to\infty$. In our case where $[\alpha]\neq 0$, the limit on the universal cover is $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$, which is different from $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
We note that in case (i), the conclusion implies $\alpha^*(\partial_v)=0$ since $\alpha^*=\sqrt 2 d\theta$, which means $\alpha^*$ has no component in the fiber direction of the action $G_\infty$. This fact plays an important role in showing the full sequence convergence both in Gromov--Hausdorff sense and on the universal cover (cf. Theorem~\ref{no_fiber_component}).
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
We want to compare our result with the result of Lott \cite{lott2010dimensional,lott2026under} where he showed that compact 3-dimensional Type III Ricci flow has similar collapsing behavior determined by the Thurston type of the manifold. Our result can be viewed as a generalization to noncompact manifolds with symmetry determined by $[\alpha]$ in view of the warped product interpretation in Section~\ref{intro}. In our case, the collapsing behavior also depends on the topology, namely the cohomology class $[\alpha]$.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- remark-7: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{t:main.GH_limit} exhibits a new type of metric behavior in a geometric flow, despite the many formal similarities between the Ricci flow and \eqref{alpha_Ricci_flow}. In particular, the long-time behavior depends on the arithmetic property of the initial data. As $[\alpha]$ varies, the long-time behavior changes dramatically. We expect that analogous phenomena occur for higher-dimensional tori as well.
<!-- end remark-7 -->
<!-- remark-8: body_md -->
The metric component of the $\alpha$-Ricci flow \eqref{alpha_Ricci_flow} is not conformal. When the initial metric is flat and the $1$-form is parallel, one can show that the conformal class $[g(t)] \in \mathbb{H}^2 / \operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{Z})$ converges to $\left[\left(\frac{a}{b}, 0\right)\right] \in \partial \mathbb{H}^2 / \operatorname{PSL}_2(\mathbb{Z})$ where $a,b$ are the periods of $\alpha$ on a set of generators of $\pi_1(M)$ (cf.\ the proof of Theorem~\ref{model.case}).
<!-- end remark-8 -->
<!-- remark-9: body_md -->
In the irrational case, our method only gives a subsequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that the desired convergences hold. It would be desirable to show that the convergences are along the full sequence.
<!-- end remark-9 -->
<!-- definition-5: body_md -->
Let $K$ be the Klein bottle.  
Let $[\alpha]\in H^1(K,\mathbb{R})\cong\mathbb{R}$. If $[\alpha]\neq 0$, we define $\ell([\alpha])$ to be the unique $\ell>0$ such that $[\alpha]=\ell v$, where $v\in H^1(K,\mathbb{Z})$ is a generator of $H^1(K,\mathbb{Z})\cong \mathbb{Z}$. If $[\alpha]=0$, we define $\ell([\alpha])=0$.
<!-- end definition-5 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{c:klein.bottle}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on the Klein bottle $K$. Then the following hold.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item If $[\alpha]\neq 0$, then $(K,t^{-1}g(t))\xrightarrow{GH} ~(S^1,d([\alpha]))$ as $t\to\infty$. On the universal cover $\tilde K$, we have $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$. Moreover, we have the following equivariant convergence
        \begin{center}
\begin{tikzcd}
  & (\tilde{K},\tilde{g}_{s}(1), \tilde\alpha_{s}(1), G_{s}, p) 
  \arrow[r, "pt\ eq\ C^\infty"] 
  \arrow[d, "\pi"]
       & (\mathbb{R}^2,g^*(1), \alpha^*(1), G_\infty, p_\infty) 
   \arrow[d, "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (K, g_{s}(1)) 
  \arrow[r, "GH"]
   & (S^1,d([\alpha]))
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
        where
        $G_s=\pi_1(K)\subset \operatorname{Isom}(\tilde g_s(1))$ and $G_\infty\cong \mathbb{R}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}\subset \operatorname{Isom}( g^*(1))$ is generated by translations in $\partial_v$ direction and the glide reflection $(\theta,v)\mapsto (\theta+\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt{2},-v)$.
    \item If $[\alpha]=0$, then
    \[
        (K,t^{-1}g(t))\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}
        \quad\text{as }t\to\infty,
    \]
    and, on the universal cover,
    \[
        (\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))
        \xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)
        \quad\text{as }s\to\infty.
    \]
    \end{enumerate}
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- remark-10: body_md -->
As in Remark~\ref{r:lott.sesum}, the case $[\alpha]=0$ follows directly from \cite{lott2014ricci}.
<!-- end remark-10 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{p:equivalent definition}
    Define $\alpha,[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2],a,b$ as above. We have the following.
    \begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
        \item $[\alpha]$ is rational  if and only if $a/b\in\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$ and $[\alpha]$ is irrational if and only if $a/b\in \mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$;
        \item $\ell_{a,b} = \ell([\alpha])$, where $\ell([\alpha])$ is defined in Definition~\ref{d:prmitive length}.
    \end{enumerate}
    In particular, for a given $[\alpha]$, the property that $a/b\in\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$ as well as the value of $\ell_{a,b}$ are independent of the choice of $[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2]\in \pi_1(\mathbb{T}^2)$.
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
To prove (i), let $[\gamma_1]^*,[\gamma_2]^*\in H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})$ be the dual basis of $[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2]\in H_1(M,\mathbb{Z})$. 
    Suppose $[\alpha]$ is rational. Then there exists $c>0$ such that:
    \begin{align*}
        c[\alpha] = m[\gamma_1]^* +n[\gamma_2]^*,
    \end{align*}
    for some integers $m,n\in\mathbb{Z}$. Thus, we have $a = m/c$ and $b = n/c$. Hence $a/b = m/n\in \mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$.  
    
    Now suppose $a/b\in\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$. If $a,b\neq0$, then there exist $p,q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}$ such that $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{p}{q}$, with $\operatorname{gcd}(p,q) = 1$ and $p/a = q/b = c$, for some constant $c$. Thus, $c[\alpha] = p[\gamma_1]^*+q[\gamma_2]^*$, which shows that $[\alpha]$ is rational. If $a = 0$ or $b = 0$, then we can choose $c = b^{-1}$ or $a^{-1}$ to see that $[\alpha]$ is rational.
    
    To prove (ii), by definition of $a,b,[\gamma_1]^*,[\gamma_2]^*$, we have $[\alpha] = a[\gamma_1]^* +b[\gamma_2]^*$. If $a/b\in \mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$, then by definition $\ell([\alpha])=\ell_{a,b}=0$. If $a/b\in\mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$ and $a,b\neq0$, there exist $p,q\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}$ such that $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{p}{q}$ with $\operatorname{gcd}(p,q) = 1$. We have \[(\ell_{a,b})^{-1}[\alpha]=\operatorname{sign}(a)|p|[\gamma_1]^*+\operatorname{sign}(b)|q|[\gamma_2]^*\in H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})\] and\begin{align*}
        \mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})=&\{c[\alpha]:ca\in \mathbb{Z},cb\in \mathbb{Z}\}\\
        =&\{c'(\ell_{a,b})^{-1}[\alpha]:c'\operatorname{sign}(a)|p|\in \mathbb{Z},c'\operatorname{sign}(b)|q|\in \mathbb{Z} \}\\
        =&\{c'(\ell_{a,b})^{-1}[\alpha]:c'\in\mathbb{Z}\},
    \end{align*} where we substitute $\ell_{a,b}c=c'$ in the second equality and use $\gcd(p,q)=1$ in the third equality. Thus $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\cdot [(\ell_{a,b})^{-1}\alpha]$ and $[\alpha]=\ell_{a,b}[(\ell_{a,b})^{-1}\alpha]$. If $a = 0$ or $b = 0$, then $\mathbb{R}\cdot[\alpha]\cap H^1(M,\mathbb{Z})=\mathbb{Z}\cdot[\gamma_2]^*$ or $\mathbb{Z}\cdot[\gamma_1]^*$ and $[\alpha]= \ell_{a,b}(\operatorname{sign}(b) [\gamma_2]^*)$ or $ \ell_{a,b}(\operatorname{sign}(a) [\gamma_1]^*)$. In any case, $\ell([\alpha])=\ell_{a,b}$.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{action.on.level.sets}
    Let $(M,g)$ be a Riemannian 2-torus, and $[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2]$ be a set of generators of $\pi_1(M)$. We write $\int_{\gamma_1}\alpha=a$, $\int_{\gamma_2}\alpha=b$. We view $[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2]$ as deck transformations which act isometrically on the universal cover $(\tilde M,\tilde g)$. On the universal cover, the lift of $\alpha$ can be written as $\tilde \alpha=du$ for some function $u$. Then for any $x\in \tilde M$  we have \[u([\gamma_1] (x))-u(x)=a\text{ and }u([\gamma_2] (x))-u(x)=b.\] 
    This is saying $\pi_1(M)$ acts on the set $\{u^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ of level sets of $u$ which is generated by $u^{-1}(r)\mapsto u^{-1}(r+a)$ and $u^{-1}(r)\mapsto u^{-1}(r+b)$. In particular,
    \begin{itemize}
        \item If $[\alpha]$ is rational, then the action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\{u^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is equivalent to the discrete $\mathbb{Z}$ action on $\{u^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ generated by $u^{-1}(r)\mapsto u^{-1}(r+\ell([\alpha]))$ for any $r\in \mathbb{R}$. In other words, $\{[\alpha](\sigma):\sigma\in \pi_1(M)\}=\mathbb{Z} \cdot \ell([\alpha])$;
        \item If $[\alpha]$ is irrational, then the action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\{u^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ has dense orbits.
    \end{itemize}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Let $\tilde\gamma_1$ be a lift of $\gamma_1$ to $\tilde M$ such that $\tilde\gamma_1(0)=x$ and $\tilde\gamma_1(1)=[\gamma_1](x)$. Then \[u([\gamma_1](x))-u(x)=\int _{\tilde\gamma_1} du(\tilde\gamma_1')=\int_{\gamma_1}\alpha=a.\] In particular, $[\gamma_1]$ maps $u^{-1}(r)$ to $u^{-1}(r+a)$ for any $r\in\mathbb{R}$. The case for $[\gamma_2]$ is similar. 

    If $[\alpha]$ is rational, by an algebraic fact, the action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\{u^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is a $\mathbb{Z}$-action generated by $u^{-1}(r)\mapsto u^{-1}(r+\ell_{a,b})$ and $\ell_{a,b}=\ell([\alpha])$ by Proposition~\ref{p:equivalent definition}. 
    
    If $[\alpha]$ is irrational, by an algebraic fact, then the action has dense orbits.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- definition-6: body_md -->
We say that $(M,g, \alpha,V)$ is an \textit{$\alpha$-Ricci expander}, if there exists a smooth vector field $V$ such that:
    \begin{align*}
        \operatorname{Ric} - \frac{1}{4}\alpha\otimes\alpha + \frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}_V g &= -\frac{1}{2}g,\\
        d_g^*\alpha + i_{V}\alpha &= 0.
    \end{align*}
     If $V = \nabla f$ for some $f\in C^\infty(M)$, then we call $(M,g, \alpha,f)$ a \textit{gradient $\alpha$-Ricci expander}.
<!-- end definition-6 -->
<!-- example-1: body_md -->
Any Ricci expander is also an $\alpha$-Ricci expander. 
An example relevant to this paper is given by 
$
(M, g, \alpha, f) = (\mathbb{R}^N, g_{\mathrm{Euc}}, 0, -\tfrac{1}{4}|v|^2),$
where $v$ denotes the standard coordinates on $\mathbb{R}^N$.
<!-- end example-1 -->
<!-- example-2: body_md -->
\label{example.noncompact invariant expander}
Consider $(M,g,\alpha,f) = (\mathbb{R}^N\times\mathbb{R}, g_{\mathbb{R}^N}+ d\theta^2, \sqrt{2}d\theta,-\frac{1}{4}|v|^2)$ where $v$ denotes the standard coordinates on $\mathbb{R}^N$ and $\theta$ denotes the standard coordinate on $\mathbb{R}$. One can check that this is a gradient $\alpha$-Ricci expander. We write $\phi_t(\theta,v) = (\theta,t^{-\frac{1}{2}}v)$ for the one parameter family of diffeomorphisms generated by $\frac{1}{t}\nabla f$. Then \begin{align*}
    g^*(t) &=t\phi_t^*g= g_{\mathbb{R}^N} + td\theta^2\\
    \alpha^*(t) &=\phi_t^*\alpha= \sqrt{2}d\theta\equiv \alpha^*
\end{align*} is an $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $(0,\infty)$.
Since $\alpha^*(t)$ is spatially constant and $g^*(t)$ is flat, one can view 
$(g^*(t), \alpha^*(t))$ as an $\alpha$-Ricci flow on any quotient of 
$\mathbb{R}^{N+1}$, for example, on $S^1\times\mathbb{R}^N$.
The case relevant to this paper is $N=1$.
<!-- end example-2 -->
<!-- remark-11: body_md -->
When converted back to the usual Ricci flow by taking a warped product with $\mathbb{R}$ (cf. section \ref{intro}), the expander $(g^*(t), \alpha^*(t))$ is isometric to $\mathbb{R}^{N}\times \mathbb{H}^2$.
<!-- end remark-11 -->
<!-- remark-12: body_md -->
The behavior of $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ is that the base metric on $S^1$ expands while the fiber metric on $\mathbb{R}^N$ remains fixed. This resembles the expander on the Sol-geometry in the 3D-Ricci flow \cite[Section 3.3.2]{lott2007long}. This also indicates that with the presence of $\alpha$, the long-time behavior of the $\alpha$-Ricci flow is determined not only by the curvature of $g$, which is flat for all $t$, but also by $\alpha$.
<!-- end remark-12 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{l:R2.invariant.solution}
    Let $(\tilde g(t), \tilde \alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{R}^2 \times (0,\infty)$ 
which is invariant under translations of $\mathbb{R}^2$. Then either $(\tilde g(t), \tilde \alpha(t))$ 
is isometric to $(g^*(t+c), \alpha^*(t+c))$ for some $c \ge 0$, or it is the static solution 
$(g_{\mathrm{Euc}}, 0)$.
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Since $(\tilde g(t), \tilde \alpha(t))$ is translation invariant, we have $\tilde g(t)$ is flat for $t\in (0,\infty)$ and $\tilde\alpha(t)$ is parallel with respect to $\tilde g(t)$. By \eqref{alpha_Ricci_flow}, $\tilde \alpha(t)\equiv\tilde\alpha$ is constant in time. If $\tilde\alpha=0$, then integrating \eqref{alpha_Ricci_flow} shows that $\tilde g(t)$ is isometric to $g_{\mathrm{Euc}}$. If $\tilde\alpha\neq0$, we choose coordinates $(\theta,v)$ such that $\tilde g(1)(\partial_v,\partial_\theta)=0$, $\tilde\alpha=\sqrt{2}d\theta$ and $\tilde g(1)=dv^2+\frac{c_0}{2}\tilde\alpha\otimes\tilde\alpha$ for some $c_0>0$. Integrating \eqref{alpha_Ricci_flow}, we get $\tilde  g(t)=dv^2+\frac{t-1+c_0}{2}\tilde\alpha\otimes \tilde \alpha$. Since $\tilde g(t)$ is a metric for $t\in (0,\infty)$, we have $c=c_0-1\ge0$. Thus $\tilde  g(t)=dv^2+\frac{t+c}{2}\tilde\alpha\otimes \tilde \alpha=g^*(t+c)$ and $\tilde \alpha(t)=\alpha^*(t+c)$.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{p:canonical_alpha_expander_attractor}
     Let $(\tilde g(t), \tilde \alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{R}^2 \times (0,\infty)$ 
that is invariant under translations of $\mathbb{R}^2$ and $\tilde{\alpha}$ is nonzero. Then $(\tilde{g}_s(t),\tilde{\alpha}_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
By Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}, there is $c\ge 0$ such that in some coordinates $(\theta,v)$ of $\mathbb{R}^2$ we have \[(\tilde{g}(t),\tilde{\alpha}(t))=(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))=(dv^2+\frac{t+c}{2}\alpha^*\otimes \alpha^*,\alpha^*=\sqrt 2d\theta).\] Consider the blow-down solution \[\tilde{g}_s(t) = \frac{1}{s}\tilde{g}(st)=\frac{1}{s}dv^2 + \frac{(st+c)}{2s}\alpha^*\otimes\alpha^*,\quad\tilde{\alpha}_s(t) \equiv \alpha^*.\] Let $\psi_s(\theta,v)=(\theta,s^{\frac{1}{2}}v)$. Then as $s\to\infty$, we have
\[\psi_s^*\tilde g_s(t)=dv^2 + \frac{(st+c)}{2s}\alpha^*\otimes\alpha^*\to g^*(t),\quad \psi_s^*\tilde \alpha_s(t)\equiv\alpha^*.\]This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{model.case}
	Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{T}^2$ with $g(0)$ flat and $\alpha(0)$ nonzero and parallel with respect to $g(0)$. Then we have the following.
	\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
		\item If $[\alpha]$ is rational, then $(M,t^{-1}g(t))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d([\alpha]))$ as $t\to\infty$. Moreover, we have $d_{GH}((M,t^{-1}g(t)),(S^1,d([\alpha])))=O(t^{-1/2})$.
		\item If $[\alpha]$ is irrational, then  $(M,t^{-1}g(t))\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}$ as $t\to\infty$.
	\end{enumerate}
    In either case, on the universal cover, we have $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$.
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{gcd}
	Suppose there exist $\ell>0$, $r,s\in \mathbb{Z}$ such that $ra+sb=\ell$. Then $(M,t^{-1}g(t))$ is contained in a  $Ct^{-1/2}$-neighborhood of $\pi(S_\ell)$ where $C=C(\ell,r,s,a,b,g(0))$ and
	\[S_\ell=\{\frac{c}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta:0\le c\le \ell\}\subset \tilde M\cong\mathbb{R}^2.\]
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
We consider the fundamental domain $F_t$ of the lattice generated by $e_1(t)$, $e_2(t)$:
	\[F_t=\{c_1e_1(t)+c_2e_2(t):0\le c_1,c_2\le 1\}.\] 
    It suffices to show that for any $p\in F_t$, $d_t(p,S_\ell):=\inf_{0\le c\le \ell}d_t(p,\frac{c}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta)\le Ct^{-1/2}$.
	Any point $p\in F_t$ can be written as $p=\frac{x}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta+\frac{y}{\sqrt 2}\partial_v$ for some $x,y\in\mathbb{R}$. By \eqref{est1} and \eqref{est2}, we have
	\[0\le x\le a+b,\quad |y|=O(t^{-1/2}).\]
	We write $x=\lfloor x/\ell\rfloor \ell+\{x/\ell\}\ell$ where $\lfloor x/\ell\rfloor$ is the integer part of $x/\ell$ and $\{x/\ell\}$ is the fractional part of $x/\ell$.
	Then we have
    \begin{align*}
	d_t\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta+\frac{y}{\sqrt 2}\partial_v,S_\ell\right)
	\le&d_t\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta+\frac{y}{\sqrt 2}\partial_v,
	\frac{\{x/\ell\}\ell}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta\right)\\
    \le&d_{t^{-1}\tilde g(t)}\left(
    \frac{x}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta+\frac{y}{\sqrt 2}\partial_v
    ,\frac{\{x/\ell\}\ell}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta
    +r\left\lfloor\frac{x}{\ell}\right\rfloor e_1(t)
    +s\left\lfloor\frac{x}{\ell}\right\rfloor e_2(t)\right)\\
    \le&(1+Ct^{-1/2})\left|
    \frac{x}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta+\frac{y}{\sqrt 2}\partial_v
    -\frac{\{x/\ell\}\ell}{\sqrt 2}\partial_\theta
    -r\left\lfloor\frac{x}{\ell}\right\rfloor e_1(t)
    -s\left\lfloor\frac{x}{\ell}\right\rfloor e_2(t)
    \right|\\
	\le&
	(1+Ct^{-1/2})\left|\left\lfloor \frac{x}{\ell} \right\rfloor\right|
	\left|
	\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
	-r\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
	-s\frac{b}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
	\right|
	+(1+Ct^{-1/2})\left|\frac{y}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_v\right|\\
    &\quad+(1+Ct^{-1/2})
    \left|\left\lfloor \frac{x}{\ell} \right\rfloor\right|
    \left(
    |r|\left|e_1(t)-\frac{a}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta\right|
    +|s|\left|e_2(t)-\frac{b}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta\right|
    \right)\\
	\le&Ct^{-1/2}.
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{dense}
  If $\lambda \in \mathbb{R} \setminus \mathbb{Q}$, then for any $y \in [0, 1]$ and any $\delta>0$, there exist $r, s \in \mathbb{Z}$ such that
  \[
    |y - r\lambda - s| < \delta.
  \]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
First, we show (i). By Proposition~\ref{p:equivalent definition}, $[\alpha]$ is rational if and only if $\frac{a}{b}\in \mathbb{Q}\cup\{\infty\}$. If $a,b>0$ and $\frac{a}{b}=\frac{p}{q}$ where $\gcd (p,q)=1$, then there exist $r,s\in\mathbb{Z}$ such that $r p+s q=1$ and hence $ra+sb=\frac{b}{q}=\ell_{a,b}$. If $a=0$ and $b>0$, then $0a+1b=b= \ell_{a,b}$. If $b=0$ and $a>0$, then $1a+0b=a= \ell_{a,b}$. In any case, we can apply Lemma~\ref{gcd} with $\ell=\ell_{a,b}$ and $r,s,a,b$ as above to get 
	\begin{equation}\label{epsilon}
		(M,t^{-1}g(t))\text{ is contained in a } Ct^{-1/2} \text{ neighborhood of }\pi(S_\ell).
	\end{equation}
    Now, we show that  there is $C=C(\ell,r,s,a,b,g(0))$ such that for any $0\le x,y\le 1$,
\begin{equation}\label{e:metric.on.S.ell}
	\left|d_t\left(
	\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
	\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
	\right)
	-
	\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\rho(x,y)\right|\le Ct^{-1/2}.
\end{equation}
	where $\rho$ is the standard metric on $S^1=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}$ of length 1. 

Let $|\cdot|$ denote the standard Euclidean norm in the $(\theta,v)$ coordinates. For $p_1,p_2\in \mathbb{R}^2$ we define
\begin{align*}
d_E\left(
p_1,
p_2
\right)\
=
\min_{m_1,m_2\in\mathbb Z}
\left|
p_1-p_2-m_1e_1(t)-m_2e_2(t)
\right|.
\end{align*}
Then by \eqref{est1} and \eqref{est2},
\begin{align*}
d_E\left(
\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
\right)=\min_{m_1,m_2\in \mathbb{Z}}\left|
\frac{(x-y)\ell-m_1a-m_2b}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
+\frac{(m_1b-m_2a)\sqrt {C_0}}{\rho\sqrt t}\partial_v
\right|.
\end{align*}
Since $\partial_\theta$ and $\partial_v$ are orthogonal, for any $m_1,m_2\in\mathbb Z$, we have
$$
\left|
\frac{(x-y)\ell-m_1a-m_2b}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
+\frac{(m_1b-m_2a)\sqrt {C_0}}{\rho\sqrt t}\partial_v
\right|
\ge
\left|
\frac{(x-y)\ell-m_1a-m_2b}{\sqrt{2}}
\right|.
$$
Hence
$$
d_E\left(
\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
\right)
\ge
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
\min_{m_1,m_2\in\mathbb Z}
\left|(x-y)\ell-m_1a-m_2b\right|=\frac{\ell}{\sqrt 2}\rho(x,y).
$$
On the other hand, let $n_1,n_2\in\mathbb Z$ be such that
$$
\left|(x-y)\ell-n_1a-n_2b\right|
=
\min_{m_1,m_2\in\mathbb Z}
\left|(x-y)\ell-m_1a-m_2b\right|=\ell\rho(x,y).
$$
Such $(n_1,n_2)$ can be chosen from $(0,0)$ or $\pm (r,s)$.
Then
\begin{align*}
d_E\left(
\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
\right)
&\le
\left|
\frac{(x-y)\ell-n_1a-n_2b}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
+\frac{(n_1b-n_2a)\sqrt {C_0}}{\rho\sqrt t}\partial_v
\right|\\
&\le
\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\rho(x,y)
+Ct^{-1/2}.
\end{align*}
Therefore,
$$
	\left|d_E\left(
	\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
	\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
	\right)
	-
	\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\rho(x,y)\right|\le Ct^{-1/2}.
$$
Since $\left|\frac{\tilde g(t)}{t}-(d\theta^2+dv^2)\right|\le Ct^{-1/2}$, the corresponding distance functions converge uniformly on the relevant fundamental domains. Hence
$$
\left|d_t\left(
\frac{x\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta,
\frac{y\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\partial_\theta
\right)
-
\frac{\ell}{\sqrt{2}}\rho(x,y)\right|\le Ct^{-1/2}.
$$

	Thus, we see that $(\pi(S_\ell),t^{-1}g(t))$ converges to the metric space $(S^1,d([\alpha]))$. Combining (\ref{epsilon}) and \eqref{e:metric.on.S.ell}, we get \[d_{GH}((M,t^{-1}g(t)), (S^1,d([\alpha])))=O(t^{-1/2}).\]
	
	Next, we show (ii). Since $\frac{a}{b}\in \mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}$, we have $b>0$. For any $0<\epsilon\le2b$ we apply Lemma~\ref{dense} with $\lambda=\frac{a}{b}$, $y=\frac{\epsilon}{2b}$, $\delta=\frac{\epsilon}{4b}$ to obtain $r,s\in \mathbb{Z}$ so that $|\frac{\epsilon}{2b}-r\frac{a}{b}-s|<\frac{\epsilon}{4b}$. Hence $|\frac{\epsilon}{2} -ra-sb|<\epsilon/4$. We apply Lemma~\ref{gcd} with $\ell=ra+sb$ where $\frac{1}{4} \epsilon\le \ell\le \frac{3}{4}\epsilon$. Then we get $(M,t^{-1}g(t))$ is contained in a $C(\epsilon)t^{-1/2}$-neighborhood of $\pi(S_\ell) \subset\pi(S_{3\epsilon/4})$. Since $\operatorname{diam}(\pi(S_{3\epsilon/4}))\le \sqrt{1+\frac{2A_0}{\rho^2t}}\frac{3\epsilon}{4\sqrt 2}$, for any $\epsilon>0$ there exists $t_0=t_0(\epsilon)>0$ so that for $t>t_0$, $\operatorname{diam}(M,t^{-1}g(t)) \le \epsilon$. Thus we have $(M,t^{-1}g(t))$ converges to a point in the Gromov--Hausdorff topology.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{alpha.norm.estimate}
    Let $(M^n,g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow. Then
    \[\frac{d}{dt}|\alpha|^2=\Delta |\alpha|^2-2|\nabla\alpha|^2-\frac{1}{2}|\alpha|^4.\]
    If $M$ is closed, then with $c_0=\sup_{M \times \{0\}} |\alpha|^2$ we have
    \[
\sup_{M \times \{t\}} |\alpha|^2 \le \ \frac{c_0}{1+\tfrac{c_0t}{2}}.\]
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
The proof follows the  arguments in \cite[Lemma 3.2, Lemma 5.6]{list2008evolution} and \cite[(2.15), (2.19)]{lott2014ricci}.
    We recall the Bochner formula for a closed 1-form:
    \begin{align*}
        \frac{1}{2}\Delta |\alpha|^2=&-\langle \Delta_d\alpha,\alpha\rangle+|\nabla\alpha|^2+\operatorname{Ric}(\alpha,\alpha)\\
        =&-\langle dd^*_g\alpha,\alpha\rangle+|\nabla\alpha|^2+\operatorname{Ric}(\alpha,\alpha).
    \end{align*}
    Consequently, we have
    \begin{align*}
	\frac{d}{dt}|\alpha|^2=&-2\langle dd^*_g\alpha,\alpha\rangle+2\operatorname{Ric}(\alpha,\alpha)-\frac{1}{2}|\alpha|^4\\
	=&\Delta|\alpha|^2-2|\nabla\alpha|^2-\frac{1}{2}|\alpha|^4.
\end{align*}
    Applying the maximum principle when $M$ is closed gives
    \[
\sup_{M \times \{t\}} |\alpha|^2 \le \ \frac{c_0}{1+\tfrac{c_0t}{2}}.\]
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{scalar.curvature.monotonicity}
    Let $(M^n,g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $[0,T]$. We define $R^{ \alpha}=R-\frac{1}{4}|\alpha|^2$ and $\operatorname{Ric}^{\alpha}=\operatorname{Ric} -\frac{1}{4}\alpha\otimes \alpha$. Then we have
    \[
\frac{\partial  R^{ \alpha}}{\partial t} = \Delta  R^{ \alpha} + 2|{\mathrm{Ric}}^{\alpha}|^2+\frac{1}{2}(d^*_g\alpha)^2.
\]
    If $M$ is closed, then for any $t\in (0,T]$
\[
\inf_{M\times\{t\}} R^{\alpha}\ge -\frac{n}{2t}.
\]
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The evolution equation for $ R^{ \alpha}$ on $M$ is proved in \cite{guo2025alpha}, see also \cite[(2.16)]{lott2014ricci}. If $M$ is closed, then using the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality $|\operatorname{Ric}^\alpha|^2\ge \frac{1}{n}(R^\alpha)^2$ and the maximum principle, we get $
\inf_{M\times\{t\}} R^{\alpha}\ge -\frac{n}{2t}$.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{volume.estimate}
	Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$ and set $c_0=\sup_{M\times\{0\}} |\alpha|^2$. Letting $V(t)=\operatorname{vol}(M,g(t))$, we have
	\[V(0)\le V(t) \le V(0)\sqrt{1+\frac{c_0t}{2}}.\]
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
By a direct computation and the Gauss-Bonnet theorem, we have
    \[V'(t)=\int (-R+\frac{1}{4}|\alpha|^2)dV_{g(t)}=\frac{1}{4}\int|\alpha|^2dV_{g(t)}.\]
    Applying Theorem~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate}, we get
    \[0\le V'(t)\le \frac{c_0}{4+2c_0t}V(t).\]
    Integration gives the claimed bounds.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{type.iii.estimate}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$. Then the flow exists for all $t\in [0,\infty)$ and there exists $C>0$ such that
	\[|R(x,t)|\le \frac{C}{1+t}.\]
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
The proof closely follows that of \cite[Proposition 2.10]{lott2014ricci}, using Theorem~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate} in place of \cite[(2.15), (2.19)]{lott2014ricci}. We provide a brief sketch here.


    We first show that there is no finite-time singularity. Suppose, for the sake of contradiction, that there is $T<\infty$ such that the flow is singular at $T$. Then by \cite{guo2025alpha}, we have $\lim_{t\to T^-}\sup_{M\times\{t\}}|R|=\infty$. We can choose a sequence $(x_i,t_i)\in M\times [0,T)$ such that $Q_i:=|R(x_i,t_i)|=\sup _{M\times[0,t_i]}|R(x,t)|\to\infty$. Define the rescaled solutions $g_i(t)=Q_i g(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t)$ and $\alpha_i(t)=\alpha(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t)$. By \cite{guo2025alpha}, $(M\times[-Q_it_i,0],(x_i,0),g_i(t),\alpha_i(t))$ converges in the pointed Cheeger-Gromov topology to $(M_\infty\times(-\infty,0],(m_\infty,0),g_\infty(t),\alpha_\infty(t))$, where $(M_\infty,g_{\infty}(t))$ is a $\kappa$-solution in the sense of \cite[\S 11.2]{perelman2002entropy} and $\alpha_\infty=0$. Hence the limit must be $(S^2,g_{S^2})$. This contradicts the assumption that $M=\mathbb{T}^2$.
    
    It remains to show that $|R(x,t)|\le Ct^{-1}$, for $t\geq 1$.
    Suppose otherwise. Then \[\limsup_{t\to\infty}\sup_{M\times\{t\}}t|R|=\infty.\] Choose a sequence $\{T_i\}_{i=1}^\infty$ with $\lim_{i\to\infty}T_i=\infty$, and let $(x_i,t_i)\in M\times[0,T_i]$ be such that 
    \[t_i(T_i-t_i)|R(x_i,t_i)|=\sup_{M\times [0,T_i]}t(T_i-t)|R(x,t)|.\]
    Setting $Q_i=|R(x_i,t_i)|$, we consider the rescaled solutions
    \[g_i(t)=Q_i g(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t),\quad \alpha_i(t)=\alpha(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t).\]
    Then $Q_it_i\to\infty$, and $(g_i(t),\alpha_i(t))$ satisfies the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on a time interval $(A_i,\Omega_i)$, where $\lim_{i\to\infty}A_i=-\infty$ and $\lim_{i\to\infty}\Omega_i=\infty$. This can be seen as in \cite[Chapter 8, 5.1]{chow2004ricci}.
    Since $|R_i(x_i,0)|=1$, we can redefine $A_i,\Omega_i$ such that there exists a sequence $\{\gamma_i\}_{i=1}^\infty$, with $\lim_{i\to\infty}\gamma_i=1$ satisfying
    \[\sup_{M\times [A_i,\Omega_i]}|R_i(x,t)|\le \gamma_i.\]
    Following the idea of the proof in \cite[Lemma 2.11]{lott2014ricci}, and applying Theorem~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate},
    there is an $\epsilon>0$ such that $\operatorname{inj} _{(M,g_i(0))}(x_i)\ge \epsilon$ for all $i\in\mathbb{N}$.
    Taking the pointed limit of $(M\times[A_i,\Omega_i],(x_i,0),g_i(t),\alpha_i(t))$, we obtain a limit $(M_\infty\times\mathbb{R},~(m_\infty,0),g_\infty(t),\alpha_\infty(t))$. By Theorem~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate},
    \[
    \sup_M|\alpha_i(t)|_{g_i(t)}^2
    =Q_i^{-1}\sup_M|\alpha(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t)|_{g(t_i+Q_i^{-1}t)}^2
    \le\frac{2}{Q_it_i+t}\longrightarrow0
    \]
    uniformly on compact time intervals, so $\alpha_\infty=0$. Theorem~\ref{scalar.curvature.monotonicity} also gives $R_i(t)\ge-\frac{1}{Q_it_i+t}$, hence $R_\infty\ge0$. Moreover, we have
    \[|R_i(x_i,0)|=1.\]
    In conclusion, $(M_\infty,g_\infty(t))$ is a complete eternal solution to the Ricci flow with uniformly bounded curvature. Since the spacetime maximum of $R$ is achieved at some point, \cite[Theorem 9.4]{chow2006hamilton} implies that $(M_\infty,g_\infty(t))$ is the cigar soliton. 
    In particular 
    \[\int_{M_\infty}R_{g_\infty(0)}dV_{g_\infty(0)}=4\pi.\]
    For sufficiently large $i$, there exists a subset $S_i\subset M$ such that
    \[\int_{S_i}R_{g(t_i)}dV_{g(t_i)}\ge 3\pi.\]
    On the other hand, by Theorem \ref{scalar.curvature.monotonicity}
    \[\int_{M\setminus S_i}R_{g(t_i)}dV_{g(t_i)}\ge -\frac{1}{t_i} \operatorname{vol}_{g(t_i)}(M\setminus S_i)\ge -\frac{V(t_i)}{t_i}\ge -\frac{C(1+\sqrt{t_i})}{t_i}\to0.\]
    Thus, for sufficiently large $i$, we obtain
    \[\int_MR_{g(t_i)}dV_{g(t_i)}\ge 2\pi,\]
    which contradicts the fact that $\chi(M)= 0$.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{growth.of.length.of.shortest.curve}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$.
	For a homology class $\sigma\in H_1(M,\mathbb{Z})$, let $[\alpha](\sigma):=\int_\sigma\alpha$. Define $L_\sigma(t):=\inf\{L(\gamma,g(t)):[\gamma]\in \sigma\}$ and
    $L(t)=\inf \{L_\sigma(t):0\neq \sigma\in H_1(M,\mathbb{Z})\}$. Then the Dini derivatives of $L_\sigma(t)$ satisfy
    \begin{equation}\label{D^+L_sigma.upper.bound}
        D^+L_\sigma(t):=\limsup_{\Delta t\to0+}\frac{L_\sigma(t+\Delta t)-L_\sigma(t)}{\Delta t}\le\int_0^L-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{4}(\alpha(\gamma'))^2ds
    \end{equation}
    and
    \[D_-L_\sigma(t):=\liminf_{\Delta t\to0+}\frac{L_\sigma(t)-L_\sigma(t-\Delta t)}{\Delta t}\ge\int_0^L-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{4}(\alpha(\gamma'))^2ds.\]
    Furthermore, $L_\sigma$ satisfies the following growth bound:
    \begin{equation}\label{growth.L_sigma}
        L_\sigma(t)^2\ge \frac{[\alpha](\sigma)^2}{2}t+L_\sigma(0)^2.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, $L(t)$ is nondecreasing in $t$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Let $\gamma$ be a closed geodesic representing the homology class $\sigma$ such that $L(\gamma,g(t))=L_\sigma(t)=:L$. Bounding $L_{\sigma}(t+\Delta t)$ by the length of $\gamma$ at time $t+\Delta t$, we obtain
    \[\limsup_{\Delta t\to0+}\frac{L_\sigma(t+\Delta t)-L_\sigma(t)}{\Delta t}\le\frac{1}{2}\int_0^L\frac{\partial g}{\partial t}(\gamma',\gamma')ds=\int_0^L-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{4}(\alpha(\gamma'))^2ds.\]
    Similarly, bounding $L_{\sigma}(t-\Delta t)$ by the length of $\gamma$ at time $t-\Delta t$ gives
    \[D_-L_\sigma(t):=\liminf_{\Delta t\to0+}\frac{L_\sigma(t)-L_\sigma(t-\Delta t)}{\Delta t}\ge\int_0^L-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{4}(\alpha(\gamma'))^2ds.\]
    Applying the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives
	\[|[\alpha](\sigma)|=\left|\int_\gamma\alpha\right|=\left|\int_0^{L} \alpha(\gamma')ds\right|\le L_\sigma(t)^{1/2}(\int \alpha(\gamma')^2ds)^{1/2}.\]
    Because $\gamma$ is a stable closed geodesic, it follows that
    \[\int_{\gamma}R(\gamma(s))ds\le 0.\] Combining the above, we deduce that
	\[D_-L_\sigma(t)\ge \int_0^L-\frac{1}{2}R+\frac{1}{4}(\alpha(\gamma'))^2ds\ge \frac{[\alpha](\sigma)^2}{4}L_\sigma(t)^{-1}.\]
    Solving this differential inequality yields
	\[L_\sigma(t)^2\ge\frac{[\alpha](\sigma)^2}{2}t+L_\sigma(0)^2.\]
    In particular, $L_\sigma(t)$ is non-decreasing in $t$. Since $L(t)=\inf_{\sigma\neq0}L_\sigma(t)$, we conclude that $L(t)$ is also non-decreasing.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{diameter.bound}
	Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$. Then the diameter satisfies
	\[\operatorname{diam}(M,g(t)) \le C(\sqrt{t}+1).\]
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Combining Theorem~\ref{volume.estimate} and Theorem~\ref{type.iii.estimate} yields
	\[\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)\sup_{x\in M} |R(x,t)|=0.\]
	It then follows from \cite[Claim 2.15]{lott2014ricci} that \[\operatorname{diam}(M,g(t))\le CL(t)^{-1}V(t)\le CL(0)^{-1}V(t)\le C(\sqrt{t}+1).\]
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
The desired conclusion follows directly from combining Theorem~\ref{volume.estimate}, Theorem~\ref{type.iii.estimate}, and Theorem~\ref{diameter.bound}.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{develop}
Let $(M_i,g_i)$ be a sequence of $2$-tori  satisfying, for each $i$,
\begin{itemize}
    \item[(a)] $|\operatorname{Rm}(g_i)|\le K$,
    \item[(b)] $\operatorname{vol} (M_i,g_i)\to0$,
    \item[(c)] $\operatorname{diam}(M_i,g_i)\leq 1$.
\end{itemize}
Assume that there exists a subsequence, still denoted by $(M_i, g_i)$, such that
\begin{align*}
   \begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
         \& (M_i,g_i) \arrow[r, "\text{GH}"] 
         \& (X, d)
\end{tikzcd}.
\end{align*}
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item $(X,d)$ is either $S^1$ with diameter bounded by $1$ or a point $\{\mathrm{pt}\}$.
    \item The universal cover is noncollapsing, in particular, up to a subsequence, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
         \& (\tilde{M_i},\tilde{g_i}, p_i) \arrow[r, "\text{pt } C^{1,\alpha}"] 
         \& (\mathbb{R}^2, g_\infty, p)
    \end{tikzcd}
    \end{align*}
\end{enumerate}
for some $C^{1,\alpha}$ metric $g_\infty$ on $\mathbb{R}^2$.
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
To prove (i), we apply
\cite[Proposition 11.5]{fukaya1990hausdorff} to see that $(X,d)$
can only be a circle, an interval, or a point.
By \cite[Theorem 4.1]{katz2020torus}, the interval case is ruled out.
The diameter estimate follows directly from
Gromov--Hausdorff convergence.

To prove (ii), we first establish a controlled contraction property
on the universal covers. Fix $\epsilon\in(0,1/2)$.
We claim that 
for all sufficiently large $i$, every loop
$\gamma$ in $\tilde M_i$, based at a point $q$, admits a based
contraction $\{\gamma_s\}_{s\in[0,1]}$ satisfying $\gamma_0=\gamma$, $\gamma_1(t)=q$ for all $t\in [0,1]$ and
\begin{equation}\label{e:controlled.loop.contraction}
    L_{\tilde g_i}(\gamma_s)
    \le e^\epsilon L_{\tilde g_i}(\gamma)
    \qquad\text{for every }s\in[0,1].
\end{equation}

Suppose first that $X$ is a circle. By the \cite{cheeger1992nilpotent}, for sufficiently
large $i$ there is an invariant metric $h_i$ on the circle
fibration $M_i\to S^1$ satisfying $e^{-\epsilon}g_i\le h_i\le e^\epsilon g_i$.
After lifting to the universal cover, choosing arclength on the
lifted base, and removing the connection term, we obtain global
coordinates $(x,y)\in\mathbb R^2$ in which $\tilde h_i=dx^2+f_i(x)^2dy^2$.
Write $q=(x_0,y_0)$. First contract the $y$-coordinate of $\gamma$
linearly toward $y_0$, keeping its $x$-coordinate fixed. Then
contract the remaining $x$-coordinate linearly toward $x_0$.
Both stages fix $q$ and do not increase $\tilde h_i$-length.
Consequently,
\[
    L_{\tilde g_i}(\gamma_s)
    \le e^{\epsilon/2}L_{\tilde h_i}(\gamma_s)
    \le e^{\epsilon/2}L_{\tilde h_i}(\gamma)
    \le e^\epsilon L_{\tilde g_i}(\gamma),
\]
which proves \eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}.

If $X$ is a point, let $d_i=\operatorname{diam}(M_i,g_i)\to0$
and normalize the metrics by $\hat g_i=d_i^{-2}g_i$.
Then
\[
    \operatorname{diam}(M_i,\hat g_i)=1,
    \qquad
    |\operatorname{Rm}(\hat g_i)|\le Kd_i^2\longrightarrow0.
\]
After passing to a subsequence, $(M_i,\hat g_i)\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d)$ or $(M_i,\hat g_i)\xrightarrow{C^{1,\alpha}}(\mathbb{T}^2,g_\infty)$.
In the former case, the preceding invariant-metric approximation
applies. In the latter case, $g_\infty$ is flat, so $\hat g_i$ is bilipschitz to a fixed flat metric $g_\infty$ for sufficiently
large $i$. Thus $\hat g_i$ satisfies \eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}. Rescaling preserves the length ratio in
\eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}, and thus the claim is proved.

We first use \eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction} to obtain a
uniform injectivity-radius bound. Choose $\delta>0$ such that $e^\epsilon\delta<\frac{\pi}{\sqrt K}$.
Suppose that $\operatorname{inj}_{\tilde g_i}(q)=r<\delta$
for some $q\in\tilde M_i$. The curvature bound implies that
there are no conjugate points at distance less than
$\pi/\sqrt K$. Thus Klingenberg's cut-locus lemma gives a
nonconstant geodesic loop based at $q$ of length $2r$.
By \eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}, it contracts through
based loops of length at most $2e^\epsilon r<\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt K},$
contradicting Klingenberg's long homotopy lemma.
Hence $\operatorname{inj}_{\tilde g_i}(q)\ge\delta$
    for every $q\in\tilde M_i$.

The universal covers are therefore noncollapsing, and standard
pointed compactness gives, after passing to a subsequence, $(\tilde M_i,\tilde g_i,p_i)
    \xrightarrow{pt\  C^{1,\alpha}}(M_\infty,g_\infty,p_\infty)$.

By \eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}, any loop in $M_\infty$
is approximated by loops in $\tilde M_i$ whose based contractions
remain in a uniformly bounded ball. Pulling these contractions
back through the convergence maps shows that the original loop
is null-homotopic. Hence $M_\infty$ is simply connected.

Minimizing geodesic rays in the universal covers pass to a ray
in $M_\infty$, so the limit is noncompact. Being also simply
connected, $M_\infty$ is diffeomorphic to $\mathbb R^2$.
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{c:convergence_CFG}
    Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$. For any sequence $s_i\to\infty$, there exists a subsequence, still denoted by $s_i$, and a limit space $(X,d)$ that is either $(S^1, d)$ or $\{\mathrm{pt}\}$, such that the following convergence holds. Choose $p \in M$ and a lift $\tilde{p} \in \tilde{M}\cong \mathbb{R}^2$ such that  $\pi(\tilde p)=p$, where $\pi:\tilde M\to M$ is the covering map. We have pointed $C^\infty$ convergence of the flow on $(0,\infty)$:
        \begin{align*}
        \begin{tikzcd}[ampersand replacement=\&]
         \& (\tilde M,\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t), p) \arrow[r, "\text{pt } C^{\infty}"] 
         \& (\mathbb{R}^2, \tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t), p).
    \end{tikzcd}
    \end{align*}
     Moreover, the time $1$ slice has the following equivariant convergence diagram:
    \begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
  & (\tilde M,\tilde{g}_{s_i}(1), \tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1),G_i,\tilde{p}) \arrow[r,  "\text{pt eq }C^\infty"] \arrow[d, "\pi"]
   & (\mathbb{R}^2,\tilde{g}_\infty(1),\tilde\alpha_\infty(1), G_\infty,\tilde{p}_\infty) \arrow[d,  "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (M, g_{s_i}(1)) \arrow[r, "\text{GH}"]
   & (X,d),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
where $G_i =\pi_1(M_i)\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}$ acts isometrically on $(\tilde M,\tilde{g}_{s_i}(1))$, $\tilde{g}_\infty(1)$ is some metric on $\mathbb{R}^2$, and $G_\infty\subset \operatorname{Isom}(\tilde{g}_\infty(t))$ preserves $\tilde \alpha_\infty(t)$  for all $t\in(0,\infty)$. Furthermore, $G_\infty\cong \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$ if $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$, and $G_\infty\cong\mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}$ if $(X,d)=\{\mathrm {pt}\}$.
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
By Theorem~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate} and Theorem~\ref{type.iii.estimate}, we have the estimates
\[
|\alpha_{s_i}(t)|_{g_{s_i}(t)}^2 \le \frac{c_0 s_i}{1 + \frac{c_0}{2} s_i t}, \qquad 
|R(g_{s_i}(t))| \le \frac{C s_i}{1+s_i t}.
\]
In particular, for any $T_0>0$, these yield uniform bounds on the curvature and on $|\alpha|$ over the time interval $[T_0,\infty)$. Therefore, the noncollapsing result in Theorem \ref{develop} and the compactness theorem in~\cite{guo2025alpha} apply and give the smooth pointed convergence on the universal cover.


    By Theorem~\ref{type.iii.estimate} and Gromov's compactness theorem \cite[Theorem 5.3]{gromov2007metric}, there exists a metric space $(X,d)$ such that $(M, g_{s_i}(1))\to (X,d)$ in the Gromov--Hausdorff topology. By Theorem~\ref{volume.estimate} and Theorem~\ref{develop}, we know that $(X,d)$ is either $(S^1, d)$ or a point $\{\mathrm{pt}\}$. The equivariant convergence and the fact that $G_\infty$ acts by isometries on $(\mathbb{R}^2,\tilde g_\infty(1))$ follow from \cite{fukaya1988boundary}.
Because $\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1)$ is invariant under $G_i$, the equivariant smooth convergence on the universal cover implies that $\tilde \alpha_\infty(1)$ is invariant under $G_\infty$. 
    
    We would like to show that $G_\infty$ acts by isometries on $(\mathbb{R}^2,\tilde g_\infty(t))$ and preserves $\tilde\alpha_\infty(t)$ for any $t\in (0,\infty)$. Fix $\gamma_\infty\in G_\infty$. By equivariant convergence at
    time one, there exist $\gamma_i\in G_i$ converging locally uniformly
    to $\gamma_\infty$.
    Since the $\gamma_i$ are time-one isometries, their differentials
    are uniformly bounded on compact sets. The identity
    \[
    \gamma_i\circ\exp_p^{\tilde g_i(1)}
    =
    \exp_{\gamma_i(p)}^{\tilde g_i(1)}\circ d\gamma_i|_p
    \]
    and smooth convergence of the metrics upgrade this convergence
    to $C^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Passing to the limit in
    \[
    \gamma_i^*\tilde g_i(t)=\tilde g_i(t),
    \qquad
    \gamma_i^*\tilde\alpha_i(t)=\tilde\alpha_i(t)
    \]
    shows that $G_\infty$ preserves both $\tilde g_\infty(t)$ and
    $\tilde\alpha_\infty(t)$ for every $t>0$.
    
    We now show that $G_\infty$ is either $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$ or $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$. For $\delta>0$ sufficiently small, consider the local fundamental group $\pi_1(M_i,x_i;\delta)$ of \cite[Definition 7.8]{fukaya1990hausdorff}. A globally null-homotopic based loop of length at most $2\delta$ lifts to a closed loop on $\tilde M_i$. By~\eqref{e:controlled.loop.contraction}, it contracts through based loops of length at most $2e^\epsilon\delta<4\delta$, so the projected contraction lies in $B(x_i,2\delta)$. Thus no globally trivial short loop remains locally nontrivial, and the local pseudogroup identifies with restrictions of the deck action of $\pi_1(M_i)$ on $\tilde M_i$. Let $H$ denote its limit pseudogroup.

    
    If $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$, then by \cite[Proposition 11.5]{fukaya1990hausdorff}, the action of $H$ is free since $X$ is a codimension one manifold. Because $G_\infty$ is locally isomorphic to $H$, the action of $G_\infty$ is free. Consequently, $\mathbb{R}^2$ is a principal $G_\infty$-bundle over $S^1$. A standard argument then shows $G_\infty \cong \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$.
    
    If $(X,d)=\{\mathrm{pt}\}$, $G_\infty$ is abelian because it is the limit of abelian groups. Furthermore, since $X$ is a point, $G_\infty$ acts transitively on $\mathbb{R}^2$. Choosing a basepoint $p\in \mathbb{R}^2$, we have $\mathbb{R}^2\cong G_\infty/K$, where $K \subset G_\infty$ is the isotropy subgroup at $p$. For any $x\in \mathbb{R}^2$, choose $g\in G_\infty$ such that $g\cdot p=x$. Then for any $k\in K$, we have $k\cdot x=k\cdot(g\cdot p)=g\cdot(k\cdot p)=g\cdot p=x$. Thus, $k$ acts trivially on $\mathbb{R}^2$. It follows that $K=\{e\}$ is trivial, and therefore $G_\infty\cong \mathbb{R}\times \mathbb{R}$ as a Lie group.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- remark-13: body_md -->
\label{fiber bundle}
If $(X,d) = \{\mathrm{pt}\}$, then on the universal cover, $(\tilde{M}_\infty,\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ is $\mathbb{R}^2$-invariant. Hence, $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ is one of the solutions given in Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}.

If $(X,d) = (S^1, d)$, then $(\tilde{M}_\infty,\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ can be viewed as an $\alpha$-Ricci flow on an $\mathbb{R}$-bundle over $S^1$ i.e., we can write
\begin{align*}
\tilde g_\infty(t) =& u(\theta,t)d\theta^2 + G(\theta,t)(dv+A(\theta,t)d\theta)^2,\\
\tilde\alpha_\infty(t)=& a(\theta,t)d\theta+b(dv+A(\theta,t)d\theta)
\end{align*}
where $\partial_\theta$ and $\partial_v$ correspond to the $S^1$ and $\mathbb{R}$ direction respectively, and $u,A$ and $G$ are functions on $S^1\times(0,\infty)$. Using tools in Sections~\ref{s:condition monotonicity},~\ref{s:GHlimit_vs_rationality} and~\ref{s:dynamic stability},  we will show in Proposition~\ref{p:base.S^1.implies.universal.cover.expander} that $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ is the expander $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.
<!-- end remark-13 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}
If $(\bar g(t),\bar \alpha(t))$ is of the form \eqref{invariant.flow} and is a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow, then $g_{\mu\nu}, G_{ij}, A^i_\mu, a_{\mu}$, and $b_i$ satisfy the following equations:
\begin{equation}\label{e:invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}
\begin{aligned}
	\frac{\partial G_{ij}}{\partial t} &= g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G^{kl}G_{kl,\mu}G_{ij,\nu}-g^{\mu\nu} G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ik}G_{jl}F_{\mu\nu}^kF_{\gamma\delta}^l+\frac{1}{2}b_ib_j, \\
	\frac{\partial A_\mu^i}{\partial t} &= -g^{\gamma\delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i-g^{\gamma\delta}G^{ij}G_{jk,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^k-\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\delta}G^{kl}G_{kl,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^i+\frac{1}{2}a_\mu b_jG^{ij}, \\
	\frac{\partial g_{\mu\nu}}{\partial t} &= -2R_{\mu\nu}+G^{ij}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}+g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+\frac{1}{2}a_\mu a_\nu, \\
	\frac{\partial a_\mu}{\partial t} &= -(d d_{g,G}^*a)_{\mu}+b_ig^{\gamma\delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i+b_ig^{\gamma\delta}G^{ij}G_{ik,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^k+\frac{1}{2}b_ig^{\gamma\delta}G^{kl}G_{kl,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^i-\frac{1}{2}a_\mu b_ib_jG^{ij}, \\
	\frac{\partial b_i}{\partial t} &= 0,
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
where 
\[
d^*_{g,G}a = -g^{\mu\nu}a_{\mu;\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{ij,\nu}a_\mu.
\]
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
The metric component of the $\alpha$-Ricci flow reads
\begin{align*}
	&\frac{d}{dt}\Big(G_{ij}(dx^i+A^i)(dx^j+A^j)+g_{\mu\nu}dx^\mu dx^\nu\Big) \\
    =&\frac{d}{dt}\bar g = -2\overline{\mathrm{Ric}} + \frac{1}{2}\bar \alpha\otimes\bar\alpha \\ 
	=&-2\bar R_{ij}(dx^i+A^i)(dx^j+A^j)-4\bar R_{i\mu}(dx^i+A^i)dx^\mu-2\bar R_{\mu\nu}dx^\mu dx^\nu \\
	&+\frac{1}{2}(a_\mu dx^\mu+b_i (dx^i +A^i))(a_\nu dx^\nu+b_j (dx^j +A^j)).
\end{align*}
The evolution equations for $\frac{\partial G_{ij}}{\partial t}$, $\frac{\partial A_\mu^i}{\partial t}$, and $\frac{\partial g_{\mu\nu}}{\partial t}$ follow from expanding both sides and comparing coefficients. Note that this requires the formulas for $\bar R_{ij}, \bar R_{i\mu}, \bar R_{\mu\nu}$ given in \cite[(4.6)]{lott2007long}. Equivalently, one can directly add the components of $\frac{1}{2}\bar\alpha\otimes\bar \alpha$ to the system in \cite[(4.9)]{lott2007long}.

Since $\bar \alpha$ is closed, we have
\begin{align*}
0 &= d\big(a_\mu dx^\mu+b_i (dx^i +A^i)\big) = a_{\mu, \nu}dx^\nu \wedge dx^\mu + b_{i,\mu} dx^\mu \wedge (dx^i+A^i) + b_i dA^i \\
&= (a_{\nu,\mu} + b_{i,\mu} A^i_\nu + \frac{1}{2}b_i F^i_{\mu\nu}) dx^\mu \wedge dx^\nu + b_{i,\mu} dx^\mu \wedge dx^i.
\end{align*}
Setting the coefficient of $dx^\mu \wedge dx^i$ to zero, we obtain 
\begin{equation}\label{db=0}
    b_{i,\mu}=0.
\end{equation} 
Hence, each $b_i$ is a constant on $B$. Using \eqref{christoffel.symbol} and \eqref{db=0}, we compute
\begin{align*}
	&d^*_{\bar g}\big(a_\mu dx^\mu+b_i (dx^i +A^i)\big) \\
	=&-g^{\mu\nu}(a_{\mu,\nu}+b_iA_{\mu,\nu}^i)+g^{\mu\nu}(\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^\gamma a_\gamma+\bar\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^ib_i)+G^{ij}\bar\Gamma_{ij}^\mu a_{\mu} \\ 
    =&-g^{\mu\nu}(a_{\mu;\nu}+b_iA^i_{\mu,\nu})+g^{\mu\nu }A_{\mu,\nu}^ib_i-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{ij,\nu}a_\mu \\
	=&-g^{\mu\nu}a_{\mu;\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{ij,\nu}a_\mu \\
	=&d^*_{g,G}a.
\end{align*}

The $1$-form component of the $\alpha$-Ricci flow reads
\begin{align*}
    &\frac{d}{dt}(a_\mu+b_iA^i_\mu) dx^\mu+\frac{db_i}{dt}dx^i \\
    =&\frac{d}{dt}\big(a_\mu dx^\mu+b_i (dx^i +A^i)\big) \\
    =&-d d^*_{\bar g}\big(a_\mu dx^\mu+b_i (dx^i +A^i)\big) \\
    =&-(d d^*_{g,G}a)_{\mu}dx^\mu,
\end{align*}
where the last equality follows from the fact that $d^*_{g,G}a$ is a function on $B$, meaning $d d^*_{g,G}a$ has no fiber components. Finally, substituting the evolution equation for $\frac{\partial A_\mu^i}{\partial t}$ into the above yields the equations for $\frac{\partial a_\mu}{\partial t}$ and $\frac{\partial b_i}{\partial t}$.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{fiber.constant.in.spacetime}
The fiber components $b_i$ of $\bar \alpha$ are constant in spacetime.
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- definition-7: body_md -->
\label{W plus functional}
Given $f \in C^\infty(B)$ and $t > 0$, we define
\begin{align*}
	&\mathcal{W}_+(G_{ij}, A^i_\mu, g_{\mu\nu}, a_\mu, b_i, f, t) \\
	=& \int_B \Big[ t \Big( |\nabla f|^2 + R - \frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu - \frac{1}{4}G^{ij}b_i b_j - \frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{li,\nu} \\
	&\quad - \frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\nu}^i F_{\gamma\delta}^j \Big) - f + n \Big] (4\pi t)^{-n/2} e^{-f} \, d\operatorname{vol}_B.
\end{align*}
<!-- end definition-7 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{l:scalar.curvature.monotonicity}
Let $(\bar g(t), \bar \alpha(t))$ be a locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow on a compact base $B$ of dimension $n$ defined for $t \in [0,T]$. Then, for all $t \in (0,T]$, we have 
\[
\bar R^{\bar\alpha}_{\min}(t) \ge -\frac{n+N}{2t}
\]
where $\bar R^{\bar\alpha}_{\min}(t)=\min_B (\bar R(t)-\frac{1}{4}|\bar\alpha(t)|^2)$.
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Theorem \ref{scalar.curvature.monotonicity} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{\partial \bar R^{\bar \alpha}}{\partial t}
&=\bar\Delta \bar R^{\bar \alpha}
+2\left|\overline{\operatorname{Ric}}
-\frac14\bar\alpha\otimes\bar\alpha\right|_{\bar g}^2
+\frac12|d_{\bar g}^*\bar\alpha|^2\ge \bar\Delta \bar R^{\bar \alpha}+\frac{2}{n+N}(\bar R^{\bar \alpha})^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Since $\bar R^{\bar\alpha}$ descends to $B$, we have
\[
\bar\Delta\bar R^{\bar\alpha}
=\Delta\bar R^{\bar\alpha}
+\frac12g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{ij,\mu}\bar R^{\bar\alpha}_{,\nu}.
\] Since $B$ is
compact, the maximum principle therefore yields
\[
\min_B \bar R^{\bar \alpha}(t)\ge-\frac{n+N}{2t}.
\]
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{volume.ratio.lower.bound.by.W_+}
If $(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\int_B e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B = 1$, then 
\begin{align*}
    \mathcal{W}_+(G_{ij}, A^i_\mu, g_{\mu\nu}, a_\mu, b_i, f, t) 
    &\ge t{\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}_{\min} + n + \frac{n}{2}\log(4\pi) - \log\big(t^{-n/2}\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t))\big) \\
    &\ge \frac{n-N}{2} + \frac{n}{2}\log(4\pi) - \log\big(t^{-n/2}\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t))\big).
\end{align*}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
Using the expression for the integrand of $\mathcal{W}_+$, we have
\begin{align*}
    &\mathcal{W}_+(G_{ij}, A^i_\mu, g_{\mu\nu}, a_\mu, b_i, f, t) \\
	= &\int_B \Big[ t \Big( \big|\nabla f + \nabla\log\sqrt{\det(G_{ij})}\big|^2 + \bar R^{\bar \alpha} \Big) - f + n \Big] (4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B \\
    \ge& t{\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}_{\min} + n - \int_B f (4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B \\
    \ge& t{\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}_{\min} + n + \frac{n}{2}\log(4\pi) - \log\big(t^{-n/2}\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t))\big),
\end{align*}
where the final inequality follows from applying Jensen's inequality to the probability measure $\frac{d\operatorname{vol}_B}{\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t))}$ using the convex function $\phi(x) = x\log x$ evaluated at the density function $\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t))(4\pi t)^{-n/2}\exp\big({-f}\big)$. Combining this estimate with Lemma~\ref{l:scalar.curvature.monotonicity} yields the second inequality in the statement.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{t:evolution.of.w+.functional}
Under the flow equations (\ref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}) and
\begin{equation}\label{e:f_equation}
	\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}=-\Delta f+|\nabla f|^2-(R-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu+\frac{n}{2t})
\end{equation}
we have
\begin{align*}
	&\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}_+(G_{ij},A^i_\mu,g_{\mu\nu},a_\mu,b_i,f,t)\\
	=&\frac{t}{2} \int_B|g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ik}G_{jl}F_{\mu\nu}^kF_{\gamma\delta}^l-g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij,\mu}f_{,\nu}+\frac{1}{2}b_ib_j|^2(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
	&+t\int_B|g^{\gamma \delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i+g^{\gamma\delta}G^{ij}G_{jk,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^k-g^{\gamma\delta}f_{,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^i-\frac{1}{2}b_ka_\mu G^{ik}|^2(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
	&+2t\int_B|R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{li,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+f_{;\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2t}g_{\mu\nu}|^2(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
	&+\frac{t}{2}\int_B|d_g^*a+i_{\nabla f}a|^2(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B+\frac{1}{4}\int_Bg^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\nu}^iF_{\gamma\delta}^j(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
	&-\frac{1}{4}\int _BG^{ij}b_ib_j(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B.
    \end{align*}
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We denote the time derivatives by $\dot{G}=\frac{d}{dt}G$, $\dot{A}=\frac{d}{dt}A$, $\dot{g}=\frac{d}{dt}g$, $\dot{a}=\frac{d}{dt}a$, and $\dot{f}=\frac{d}{dt}f$, and we write $u=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}$. Differentiating with respect to $t$ and integrating by parts to eliminate derivatives on the time-differentiated terms as in \cite[Proposition 5.3]{kleiner2008notes} and \cite[Proposition 4.64]{lott2010dimensional}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{e:compute.d/dt.W+}
    \begin{aligned}
	&\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}_+(G_{ij},A_\mu^i,g_{\mu\nu},a_\mu,b_i,f,t)\\
	=&\frac{t}{2}\int_B \dot G_{kl}G^{ik}G^{jl}\left(g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu} G^{pq}G_{ip,\mu}G_{jq,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ip}G_{jq}F_{\mu\nu}^pF_{\gamma\delta}^q\right.\\
    & \quad\left.-g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij,\mu}f_{,\nu}+\frac{1}{2}b_ib_j\right)u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B-\frac{t}{2}\int_B (\dot a_\nu+b_j\dot A^j_\nu) a_\mu g^{\mu\nu} u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B   \\
    &+t\int_B \dot A_\nu^j g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij}(-g^{\gamma\delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i-g^{\gamma\delta}G^{im}G_{mk,\delta}F_{\mu\gamma}^k+g^{\gamma\delta}f_{,\delta}F_{\mu\gamma}^i+\frac{1}{2}a_\mu b_kG^{ik} )u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
    &+t\int_B \dot g^{\mu\nu}\left(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G_{jk,\mu}G^{ij}G_{il,\nu}G^{kl}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+f_{;\mu\nu}\right)u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
    &+\int_B(\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}\dot g_{\mu\nu}-\dot f-\frac{n}{2t})\left[t\left(2\Delta f-|\nabla f|^2+R-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}b_ib_j\right.\right.\\
	& \quad\left.\left.-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{li,\nu}-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\nu}^iF_{\gamma\delta}^j\right)-f+n\right]u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B\\
    &+\int_B \left(|\nabla f|^2+R-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}b_ib_j-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{li,\nu}\right.\\
    & \quad \left.-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\nu}^iF_{\gamma\delta}^j\right)u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B-\int_B \dot f u\, d\operatorname{vol}_B
    \end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Modifying the equations \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow} and \eqref{e:f_equation} by a Lie derivative with respect to $-\nabla f$,  we get
\begin{equation}\label{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow.with.f}
    \begin{aligned}
	\frac{\partial G_{ij}}{\partial t}=&g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu} G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu\delta}G_{ik}G_{jl}F_{\mu\nu}^kF_{\gamma\delta}^l+\frac{1}{2}b_ib_j-g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij,\mu}f_{,\nu}\\
	\frac{\partial A_\mu^i}{\partial t}=&-g^{\gamma\delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i-g^{\gamma\delta}G^{ij}G_{jk,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^k+\frac{1}{2}a_\mu b_jG^{ij}+g^{\gamma\delta}f_{,\delta}F_{\mu\gamma}^i\\
	\frac{\partial g_{\mu\nu}}{\partial t}=&-2R_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}+g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+\frac{1}{2}a_\mu a_\nu-2f_{;\mu\nu}\\
	\frac{\partial a_\mu}{\partial t}=&-(d(d_{g}^*a+i_{\nabla f}a))_{\mu}+b_ig^{\gamma\delta}F_{\mu\gamma;\delta}^i+b_ig^{\gamma\delta}G^{ij}G_{ik,\gamma}F_{\mu\delta}^k-\frac{1}{2}a_\mu b_ib_jG^{ij}-b_{i}g^{\gamma\delta}f_{,\delta}F_{\mu\gamma}^i\\
	\frac{\partial b_i}{\partial t}=&0\\
	\frac{\partial f}{\partial t}=&-\Delta f-(R-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu+\frac{n}{2t}).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Since \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow} and \eqref{e:f_equation} differ from \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow.with.f} only by a Lie derivative term, and since $\mathcal{W}_+$ is invariant under pullback by diffeomorphisms of $B$, the evolution of $\mathcal{W}_+$ is the same. Notice that from \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow.with.f}, we have $\dot a+b_i\dot A^i=-d(d_{g}^*a+i_{\nabla f}a)$. Substituting \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow.with.f} into \eqref{e:compute.d/dt.W+} and integrating by parts yields the desired monotonicity formula.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- remark-14: body_md -->
The terms on the right-hand side of Theorem~\ref{t:evolution.of.w+.functional} are nonnegative, except for the last one, which depends on the fiber component of $\alpha$. We will show in Section~\ref{s:no.fiber} that for blow-down limits of an $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{T}^2$ when $[\alpha]$ is rational, the fiber component vanishes in the limit.
<!-- end remark-14 -->
<!-- definition-8: body_md -->
We say that a locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow $(\bar g(t), \bar\alpha(t))$ \textit{has no fiber component} if
    $b_i=0$ for all $i=1,\dots,N$, where $b_i$ are defined in \eqref{invariant.flow}. Note that by Corollary~\ref{fiber.constant.in.spacetime}, each $b_i$ is a constant in spacetime.
<!-- end definition-8 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{c:conditional.monotonicity}
    Let $(M, \bar g(t), \bar\alpha(t))$ be a locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow defined on the time interval $(0,\infty)$. Suppose $\bar \alpha(t)$ has no fiber component and that $f$ is a solution to \eqref{e:f_equation} on $(0,\infty)$. Then $\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}_+\ge0$.

    If, in addition, $\mathcal{W}_+$ is constant, then the following properties hold:
    
    (i) $F_{\mu\nu}^i=0$.
	
	(ii) $\det(G_{ij})$ is constant in spacetime.
    
    (iii) $G_{ij}$, $A_\mu^i$, $a_\mu$ are constant in time and $g_{\mu\nu}(t)=tg_{\mu\nu}(1)$.
	
	(iv) The following equations hold
    \begin{align*}
		g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu}=&0,\\
		R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}+\frac{1}{2t}g_{\mu\nu}=&0.
	\end{align*}
    
    (v) The $1$-form $a$ is harmonic on $B$:
\[
da=0,\qquad d_g^*a=0.
\]
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
Since $\bar \alpha(t)$ has no fiber component, $\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{W}_+\ge0$ follows from Theorem~\ref{t:evolution.of.w+.functional}.
    
    If $\mathcal{W}_+$ is constant, then by Theorem~\ref{t:evolution.of.w+.functional} we have 
    \begin{align}
        &F_{\mu\nu}^i=0,\label{W+.F.mu.nu.=0}\\
	    &g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij,\mu}f_{,\nu}=0,\label{W+.Gij=0}\\
        &R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}a_\mu a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{li,\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+f_{;\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2t}g_{\mu\nu}=0,\label{W+g.mu.nu.=0}\\
        &d_g^*a+i_{\nabla f}a=0.\label{W+d^*_fa=0}
	\end{align}
	Multiplying \eqref{W+.Gij=0} by $G^{ij}$ and summing over indices gives 
	\[\Delta\log \det (G_{ij})-\langle \nabla \log \det (G_{ij}),\nabla f\rangle=0.\]
	Integrating the left-hand side against $\log(\det (G_{ij}) )e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B$ and applying the integration by parts formula, we get $\int_B|\nabla \log\det(G_{ij})|^2 e^{-f}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B=0$. Thus
    \begin{equation}\label{nabla.log.det.G=0}
	    G^{ij}G_{ij,\mu}=(\nabla\log\det (G_{ij}))_\mu=0.
	\end{equation} Hence $\det (G_{ij})$ is a constant in space. In particular
	\begin{equation}\label{Delta.log.det.G=0}
	    G^{ij}(g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}G_{ik,\mu}G^{kl}G_{lj,\nu})=\Delta \log\det(G_{ij})=0.
	\end{equation}
    Plugging \eqref{W+.F.mu.nu.=0}, \eqref{W+d^*_fa=0}, \eqref{nabla.log.det.G=0} and \eqref{Delta.log.det.G=0} into \eqref{e:invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}, we have
    \begin{equation}\label{evolve.by.L.nabla.f}
        \frac{\partial G_{ij}}{\partial t}=g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij,\mu}f_{,\nu},\quad\frac{\partial A^i_\mu}{\partial t}=0,\quad \frac{\partial g_{\mu\nu}}{\partial t}=2f_{;\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{t}g_{\mu\nu},\quad\frac{\partial a_\mu}{\partial t}=(di_{\nabla f}a)_\mu.
    \end{equation}
	In particular
    $\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\log\det G_{ij}=G^{ij}\frac{\partial G_{ij}}{\partial t}=0$ by \eqref{nabla.log.det.G=0}. Hence $\det (G_{ij})$ is constant in spacetime. Moreover, from (\ref{evolve.by.L.nabla.f}) the flow evolves by a diffeomorphism generated by $\nabla f$ and a scaling of $g_{\mu\nu}$. Thus we have
	\[\frac{\partial \bar R^{\bar \alpha}}{\partial t}=\langle\nabla f,\nabla \bar R^{\bar \alpha}\rangle-\frac{\bar R^{\bar \alpha}}{t}.\]
Since $F=0$, $b=0$, and $\det G$ is spatially constant,
$\bar R_{i\mu}=0$,
$\bar\Delta\bar R^{\bar\alpha}
=\Delta\bar R^{\bar\alpha}$, and
$d_{\bar g}^*\bar\alpha=d_g^*a$.
The evolution equation consequently gives
\[
\frac{\partial\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}{\partial t}
=
\Delta\bar R^{\bar\alpha}
+2|\bar R_{ij}|^2
+2\left|\bar R_{\mu\nu}-\frac14a_\mu a_\nu\right|^2
+\frac12(d_g^*a)^2.
\]
Combining this with
\[
\frac{\partial\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}{\partial t}
=
\langle\nabla f,\nabla\bar R^{\bar\alpha}\rangle
-\frac{\bar R^{\bar\alpha}}{t}
\]
and completing the square yields
\begin{equation}\label{equation.for.R.alpha+n/2t}
\begin{aligned}
&\Delta\left(\bar R^{\bar\alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\right)
+2|\bar R_{ij}|^2
+2\left|\bar R_{\mu\nu}-\frac14a_\mu a_\nu
+\frac{1}{2t}g_{\mu\nu}\right|^2\\
&\qquad
+\frac12(d_g^*a)^2
-\frac1t\left(\bar R^{\bar\alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\right)
=
\left\langle\nabla f,
\nabla\left(\bar R^{\bar\alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\right)
\right\rangle.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
At a negative minimum of
$\bar R^{\bar\alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}$, the left-hand side
would be strictly positive while the right-hand side vanishes.
Thus we must have $\min_B \bar R^{\bar \alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\ge 0$.
	
    On the other hand, plugging (\ref{W+.F.mu.nu.=0}) and (\ref{Delta.log.det.G=0}) into \cite[(4.6),(4.7)]{lott2007long}, we have 
    \begin{align*}
        \bar R_{ij}=&-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G_{ij;\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}G^{kl}G_{ik,\mu}G_{jl,\nu},\quad\bar R_{i\mu}=0,\\
	\bar R_{\mu\nu}=&R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu},\\
	\bar R=&R-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}.
    \end{align*}
	Tracing (\ref{W+g.mu.nu.=0}) by $g^{\mu\nu}$, we get 
    \begin{equation}\label{R.mu.f=0}
	    \bar R^{\bar \alpha}+\Delta f+\frac{n}{2t}=R-\frac{1}{4}|a|^2-\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}+\Delta f+\frac{n}{2t}=0.
	\end{equation}
	Then by the divergence theorem, we have \[\int_B (\bar R^{\bar \alpha} +\frac{n}{2t})\, d\operatorname{vol}_B=-\int_B\Delta f\, d\operatorname{vol}_B=0.\]
	Since $\min_B\bar R^{\bar \alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\ge0$, we have $\bar R^{\bar \alpha}+\frac{n}{2t}\equiv0$. Hence, by (\ref{R.mu.f=0}) and the fact that $B$ is closed, we conclude that $f$ is a constant. Due to (\ref{evolve.by.L.nabla.f}), we have (iii). By \eqref{W+d^*_fa=0}, we have (v). By (\ref{equation.for.R.alpha+n/2t}), we have $\bar R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}a_\mu a_\nu+\frac{1}{2t}g_{\mu\nu}=\bar R_{ij}=0$ which gives the equations in (iv).
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- definition-9: body_md -->
\label{d:convergence.invariant.alpha.RF}
    Let $\{(M_i, g_i(t),\alpha_i(t), p_i)\}_{i=1}^\infty$ be a sequence of locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow solutions defined on the time interval $t \in (0,\infty)$. We say that
\[ \lim_{i\to\infty} (M_i, g_i(t),\alpha_i(t), p_i)= (M_\infty, g_\infty(t),\alpha_\infty(t), p_\infty)
\]
if there are:

\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item A sequence of open subsets $\{U_j\}_{j=1}^\infty$ of $B_\infty$, each containing $p_\infty$, such that any compact subset of $B_\infty$ eventually lies in all $U_j$, and
    
    \item Open subsets $V_{i,j} \subset B_i$ containing $p_i$ and isomorphisms
    \[
    \phi_{i,j} : \pi_\infty^{-1}(U_j) \to \pi_i^{-1}(V_{i,j})
    \]
    sending $\pi_\infty^{-1}(p_\infty)$ to $\pi_i^{-1}(p_i)$, such that
    
    \item For all $j$,
    \[
    \lim_{i \to \infty} \phi_{i,j}^* g_i(t) = g_\infty(t),\lim_{i \to \infty} \phi_{i,j}^* \alpha_i(t) = \alpha_\infty(t)
    \]
    smoothly on $\pi_\infty^{-1}(U_j) \times \left[ j^{-1},\,  j\right]$.
\end{enumerate}

If $B_\infty$ is compact, then we can remove the reference to basepoints.
<!-- end definition-9 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{alpha.expander}
    Let $(\bar g(t),\bar \alpha(t))$ be a locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow with a compact base $B$ defined on the time interval $(0,\infty)$ and $\lim_{i\to\infty} (\bar g_{s_i}(t),\bar\alpha_{s_i} (t))=(\hat g(t),\hat \alpha(t))$ for some sequence $s_i\to\infty$ or $s_i\to0$. If $\bar\alpha(t)$ has no fiber component and $(\hat g(t),\hat \alpha(t))$ is locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant with a compact base $\hat B$ defined for $(0,\infty)$, then, writing $(\hat g(t),\hat \alpha(t))=(\hat G_{ij}( t),\hat A_\mu^i( t),\hat g_{\mu\nu}( t),\hat a_\mu( t),0)$, we have 
	
	(i) $\hat F_{\mu\nu}^i=0$,
	
	(ii) $\det(\hat G_{ij})$ is constant in spacetime,
    
    (iii) $\hat G_{ij}$, $\hat A_\mu^i$, $\hat a_\mu$ are constant in time and $\hat g_{\mu\nu}(t)=t\hat g_{\mu\nu}(1)$,
	
	(iv) The following equations hold
    \begin{align}
		\hat g^{\mu\nu}\hat G_{ij;\mu\nu}-\hat g^{\mu\nu}\hat G^{kl}\hat G_{ik,\mu}\hat G_{jl,\nu}=&0,\label{alpha.expander.G.ij}\\
		\hat R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{4}\hat a_\mu \hat a_\nu-\frac{1}{4}\hat G^{ij}\hat G_{jk,\mu}\hat G^{kl}\hat G_{il,\nu}+\frac{1}{2t}\hat g_{\mu\nu}=&0.\label{alpha.expander.R.mu.nu}
	\end{align}
    
    (v) The $1$-form $\hat a$ is harmonic on $\hat B$:
\[
d\hat a=0,\qquad d_{\hat g}^*\hat a=0.
\]
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
By Corollary~\ref{c:conditional.monotonicity}, it suffices to construct a solution to \eqref{e:f_equation} along $(\hat g(t), \hat \alpha(t))$ on $(0,\infty)$ for which $\mathcal{W}_+$ remains constant. We follow similar ideas in \cite[Proposition 4.39]{lott2010dimensional}.

We write $u=(4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-f}$. Then \eqref{e:f_equation} is equivalent to 
\begin{equation}\label{e:u_equation}
    \left(\frac{\partial }{\partial t}+\Delta-R+\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}G^{ij}G_{jk,\mu}G^{kl}G_{il,\nu}+\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\gamma\delta}G_{ij}F_{\mu\gamma}^iF_{\nu\delta}^j+\frac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}a_\mu a_\nu\right)u=0.
\end{equation}
Note that if $u$ is a solution to \eqref{e:u_equation}, then $\int_B u  \,d\operatorname{vol}_B$ is constant in $t$. Choose $\delta_j\downarrow0$ and $t_j\uparrow\infty$ with $\delta_j<t_j$, so that $[\delta_j,t_j]$ exhausts $(0,\infty)$. Let $\tilde{u}_j(t)$ be a solution to \eqref{e:u_equation} on the interval $[\delta_j,t_j]$ with terminal condition
\[
\tilde{u}_j(t_j) = \frac{1}{\operatorname{vol}(B, g_{\mu\nu}(t_j))}.
\]
For any $0<\delta<T$, we claim that a subsequence of the $\tilde{u}_j$'s converges smoothly on the time interval $[\delta,T]$.

To see this, at time $T+1$ we know that if $\delta_j<\delta$ and $t_j\ge T+1$ then $\tilde{u}_j(T+1) \ge 0$ and
\[
\int_B \tilde{u}_j(T+1)\, d\operatorname{vol}_B = 1.
\]
Solving the conjugate heat equation with terminal data at time $T+1$, and restricting the solution to the time interval $[\delta,T]$, gives a smoothing operator from the space of terminal data
\[
\left\{ \tilde{u} \in L^1(B) : \tilde{u} \ge 0,\ \int_B \tilde{u}\, d\operatorname{vol}_B(T+1) = 1 \right\}
\]
to $C^\infty([\delta,T]\times B)$ by the heat kernel estimate \cite{chau2011pseudolocality}. Thus we have the derivative bounds needed to extract a subsequence of the $\tilde{u}_j$'s that converges smoothly on $[\delta,T]$. By a diagonal argument over $[1/k,k]$, $k\ge2$, we can extract a subsequence of the $\tilde{u}_j$'s that converges smoothly on compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$ to a nonzero solution $\bar{u}(t)$ of \eqref{e:u_equation}, defined for $t \in (0,\infty)$.

One can show, as in \cite[Proof of Proposition 7.5]{kleiner2008notes}, that $\bar{u}(t) > 0$. If $\bar f(t)$ is given by
\[
\bar u(t) = (4\pi t)^{-n/2} e^{-\bar{f}(t)},
\]
then $\mathcal{W}_+(\bar G_{ij},\bar A^i_\mu,\bar g_{\mu\nu},\bar a_\mu,0,\bar f,t)$ is non-decreasing in $t$. We write
\[
\mathcal{W}_{+,\infty} = \lim_{t \to \infty} \mathcal{W}_+(\bar G_{ij}(t), \bar A^i_{\mu}(t), \bar g_{\mu\nu}(t),\bar a_\mu(t),0, \bar f(t),t),
\]
if we are in the case $s_i\to\infty$ and 
\[
\mathcal{W}_{+,\infty} = \lim_{t \to0} \mathcal{W}_+(\bar G_{ij}(t), \bar A^i_{\mu}(t), \bar g_{\mu\nu}(t),\bar a_\mu(t),0, \bar f(t),t),
\]
if we are in the case $s_i\to0$.
Note that $\mathcal{W}_{+,\infty}$ is possibly $\pm\infty$ for the moment.

Next, we put 
$(\bar g_{s_i}(t),\bar \alpha_{s_i}(t))=(s_i^{-1}\bar G_{ij}(s_i t),\bar A_\mu^i(s_i t),s_i^{-1}\bar g_{\mu\nu}(s_i t),\bar a_\mu(s_i t),0)$ and $u_i(t) = s_i^{n/2}\bar{u}(s_it)$. By assumption, $\lim_{i \to \infty} (\bar g_{s_i}(t),\bar\alpha_{s_i}(t)) = (\hat g(t),\hat\alpha(t))$ in the sense of Definition~\ref{d:convergence.invariant.alpha.RF}. Then by the same smoothing argument as above, there is a subsequence of $\{u_i(t)\}_{i=1}^\infty$ that converges smoothly on compact subsets of $(0,\infty)$ to a solution $\hat u(t)$ on $\hat B$. Define $\hat f(t)$ by
\[
\hat u(t) = (4\pi t)^{-n/2}e^{-\hat f(t)}.
\]
Then after passing to a subsequence of $s_i$,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
&\mathcal{W}_+(\hat G_{ij}(t), \hat A^i_{\mu}(t), \hat g_{\mu\nu}(t),\hat a_\mu(t),0, \hat f(t),t)\\
