=& \lim_{i \to \infty} \mathcal{W}_+(s_i^{-1}\bar G_{ij}(s_i t),\bar A_\mu^i(s_i t),s_i^{-1}\bar g_{\mu\nu}(s_i t),\bar a_\mu(s_i t),0,\bar f(s_i t),t) \\
=& \lim_{i \to \infty} \mathcal{W}_+(\bar G_{ij}(s_i t),\bar A_\mu^i(s_i t),\bar g_{\mu\nu}(s_i t),\bar a_\mu(s_i t),0,\bar f(s_i t),s_it) \\
=& \mathcal{W}_{+,\infty}
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}

This shows that $\mathcal{W}_{+,\infty}< \infty$ and that
\[
\mathcal{W}_+(\hat G_{ij}(t), \hat A^i_{\mu}(t), \hat g_{\mu\nu}(t),\hat a_\mu(t),0, \hat f(t),t)
\]
is constant in $t$. This finishes the proof.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{1d.alpha.expander}
Let $(\bar g(t),\bar\alpha(t))$ be a locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $S^1$, defined for $t\in(0,\infty)$, with trivial linear holonomy and no fiber component. Let $s_i\to0$ or $s_i\to\infty$. After time-independent identifications of the bases and constant linear changes of the fiber coordinates, write
\[
\begin{aligned}
g_i(t):=s_i^{-1}\bar g(s_it)
&=u_i(t)(dx^0)^2+(G_i)_{jk}(t)(dx^j+A_i^j(t))(dx^k+A_i^k(t)),\\
\alpha_i(t):=\bar\alpha(s_it)&=a_i(t)\,dx^0.
\end{aligned}
\]
Suppose that $\hat G>0$, $\hat u>0$, and
\[
(G_i,u_i,a_i)\longrightarrow(\hat G,\hat u,\hat a)
\quad\text{in }C^\infty(S^1\times[\delta,T]),
\qquad 0<\delta<T<\infty.
\]
Then, choosing $x^0$ to be arclength at time one, a constant linear change of the fiber coordinates, and an orientation of $S^1$, we have
\[
\hat G_{ij}=\delta_{ij},\qquad \hat u(t)=t,\qquad \hat a=\sqrt2.
\]
In particular, for any time-independent flat connection $\hat A$, the metric and $1$-form determined by $(\hat G,\hat A,\hat u,\hat a,0)$ lift to a flow isometric to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ on the universal cover.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
Since $\dim B=1$ and $b=0$, we have $F=0$, and the equations for $G,u,a$, equation~\eqref{e:f_equation}, and $\mathcal{W}_+$ are independent of $A$. Moreover, $\mathcal{W}_+$ is unchanged by constant linear changes of the fiber coordinates. Thus the conjugate heat construction and passage to the limit in the proof of Corollary~\ref{alpha.expander} apply to the assumed convergence of $(G_i,u_i,a_i)$: the limiting $\mathcal{W}_+$ is constant, and we obtain~\eqref{alpha.expander.G.ij},~\eqref{alpha.expander.R.mu.nu}, $\partial_t\hat G=\partial_t\hat a=0$, and $\hat u(t)=t\hat u(1)$. Choose $x^0$ to be arclength at time one, so that $\hat u(t)=t$. By triviality of the linear holonomy, $\hat G_{ij}$ and $\hat a$ are periodic functions of $x^0$.
    Then equation (\ref{alpha.expander.G.ij}) becomes
    \[\hat G_{ij}''-\hat G^{kl}\hat G_{ik}'\hat G_{jl}'=0\] where $'=\partial_{0}$.
    In other words, $(\hat G^{-1}\hat G')'=0$. Thus $\hat G^{-1}\hat G'=X$ for some constant matrix $X$. Integrating $\hat G'=\hat GX$, we get $\hat G(x^0)=\hat G(0)\exp(x^0X)$. We can choose coordinates such that $\hat G(0)=I$. Then $\hat G'(0)=X$ is symmetric and in particular, $X$ is diagonalizable and has real eigenvalues. Since $\hat G$ is periodic in $x^0$, we must have $X=0$ and hence $\hat G=I$.
    Equation (\ref{alpha.expander.R.mu.nu}) now reads
	\[
		-\frac{1}{4}\hat a^2+\frac{1}{2}=0.
	\]
	Hence adjusting the orientation of $S^1$ if necessary, we have $\hat a=\sqrt 2$.
Finally, on the universal cover, write $\hat A^i=d\psi^i$; the time-independent change $y^i=x^i+\psi^i$ removes $\hat A$ and gives the asserted isometry.
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- remark-15: body_md -->
\label{r:connection.convergence}
Corollary~\ref{1d.alpha.expander} does not require $A_i\to\hat A$. Even though $F_i=0$, normalization of the fiber coordinates may make $A_i$ unbounded, so convergence of $(G_i,u_i,a_i)$ need not imply convergence in the sense of Definition~\ref{d:convergence.invariant.alpha.RF}. In the groupoid setting, different flat connections with the same $G$ and base metric give equivalent Riemannian groupoids by~\cite[Lemma~5.11]{lott2010dimensional}, although the global bundle metrics need not be isometric. To avoid groupoid language, we use convergence of $(G_i,u_i,a_i)$ directly; since $F=b=0$, these are precisely the coefficients needed in the entropy argument.
<!-- end remark-15 -->
<!-- definition-10: body_md -->
\label{tuple.equivalence}
The equivalence class $[\mathbf{\bar g}]$ consists of the tuples
\[
\left(
S^T( G\circ\phi)S,\,
( u\circ\phi)(\phi')^2,\,
\eta\circ\phi+\sqrt2(\phi-x^0)-c
\right),
\]
where $S\in\operatorname{GL}(N,\mathbb{R})$, $c\in\mathbb{R}$, and $\phi$ is a smooth orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $S^1$. In the expression $\phi-x^0$, we use a degree-one lift of $\phi$ to $\mathbb{R}$, denoted by the same symbol, satisfying $\phi(x^0+\ell)=\phi(x^0)+\ell$. Thus $\phi-x^0$ is $\ell$-periodic and defines a real-valued function on $S^1$. Changing the lift adds an integer multiple of $\ell$, which is absorbed into the constant $c$. The change in $\eta$ is obtained by
\[
\phi^*\bar\alpha
=\sqrt 2d\phi+d(\eta\circ\phi)=\left(\sqrt2+\partial_0\bigl(\eta\circ\phi
+\sqrt2(\phi-x^0)-c\bigr)\right)dx^0.
\]
For a finite-dimensional vector space $V$ and nonintegral $r>0$, let $h^r(S^1;V)$ be the closure of $C^\infty(S^1;V)$ in $C^r(S^1;V)$, with the inherited norm. Set $\mathbb V=\operatorname{Sym}_N\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$, where $\operatorname{Sym}_N$ is the space of real symmetric $N\times N$ matrices. For nonintegral $r>1$, define
\begin{equation}\label{eq:distance.to.expander.class}
 d_r([\mathbf{\bar g}],[\mathbf g^*])
 =\inf_{\mathbf v\in[\mathbf{\bar g}]}
 \|\mathbf v-\mathbf g^*\|_{h^r(S^1;\mathbb V)}.
\end{equation}
Fix $0<\rho<\theta<1$. For $\delta,\epsilon>0$, define
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathcal V_\delta
&=\{[\mathbf{\bar g}]:d_{1+\theta}([\mathbf{\bar g}],[\mathbf g^*])<\delta\},\\
\mathcal U_\epsilon
&=\{[\mathbf{\bar g}]:d_{1+\rho}([\mathbf{\bar g}],[\mathbf g^*])<\epsilon\}.
\end{aligned}
\]
We have
\[
d_r([\mathbf{\bar g}],[\mathbf g^*])=0
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
[\mathbf{\bar g}]=[\mathbf g^*].
\]
Indeed, suppose that representatives corresponding to $(S_j,\phi_j,c_j)$ converge to $(I,1,0)$ in $h^r$. Since $G>0$ and $u>0$, this convergence implies that $S_j$ and $S_j^{-1}$ are bounded and $0<c\le\phi_j'\le C$ for constants independent of $j$. After normalizing the degree-one lifts by $\phi_j(0)\in[0,\ell]$ and passing to a subsequence, $S_j\to S\in\operatorname{GL}(N,\mathbb{R})$ and $\phi_j\to\phi$ in $C^1$, where $\phi$ is a smooth orientation-preserving circle diffeomorphism. Since each $\phi_j$ is onto, convergence of the first component gives $S^T G S=I$ at every point. Write $ a=\sqrt2+\partial_0\eta$. Convergence of the second and third components gives $ a>0$ and
\[
\left(\frac{ a^2}{ u}\right)\circ\phi=2,
\qquad\text{hence}\qquad
 u=\frac12 a^2.
\]
Thus $y=x^0+\eta(x^0)/\sqrt2$ defines an orientation-preserving diffeomorphism of $S^1$, in which $\bar\alpha=\sqrt2\,dy$ and the base metric is $dy^2$. Together with the constant fiber change $S$, this gives the tuple $(I,1,0)$ after choosing the additive constant. The converse follows directly from~\eqref{eq:distance.to.expander.class}. In particular,
\[
\bigcap_{\epsilon>0}\mathcal U_\epsilon=\{[\mathbf g^*]\}.
\]
<!-- end definition-10 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{p:base.S^1.implies.universal.cover.expander}
	Let $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ be a solution to the $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $M=\mathbb{T}^2$. Let $s_i\to\infty$  be a sequence such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} (S^1,d)$ and $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ with no fiber component. Then $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))=(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, the limit is $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$-invariant and descends to an $\mathbb{R}$-invariant flow $(\bar g(t),\bar\alpha(t))$ over $B=S^1$. Write its coefficients as $G,u,a$, with base metric $u(t)d\theta^2$ and $\bar\alpha(t)=a(t)d\theta$. By Theorems~\ref{diameter.bound},~\ref{alpha.norm.estimate}, and~\ref{type.iii.estimate},
\begin{equation}\label{type.3.estimates.blow.down}
\operatorname{diam}(B,u(t)d\theta^2)\le C\sqrt t,\qquad
|R(\bar g(t))|\le \frac Ct,\qquad
|\bar\alpha(t)|^2\le \frac2t.
\end{equation}
As in the proof of Corollary~\ref{alpha.expander}, construct a normalized solution of~\eqref{e:f_equation} on compact positive-time intervals and pass to a diagonal limit on $(0,\infty)$. Lemma~\ref{volume.ratio.lower.bound.by.W_+} and Corollary~\ref{c:conditional.monotonicity} give
\[
\frac12\log(4\pi)-\log\bigl(t^{-1/2}\operatorname{vol}(B,u(t)d\theta^2)\bigr)
\le\mathcal{W}_+(t)\le\mathcal{W}_+(1),\qquad 0<t\le1.
\]
Consequently, by~\eqref{type.3.estimates.blow.down},
\begin{equation}\label{eq:base.length.backward.bounds}
0<c\le t^{-1/2}\operatorname{vol}(B,u(t)d\theta^2)
=2t^{-1/2}\operatorname{diam}(B,u(t)d\theta^2)\le C,
\qquad 0<t\le1.
\end{equation}
In particular, $\mathcal{W}_+$ is bounded from below on $(0,1]$.

Let $t_i\to0$ and write $(g_i(t),\alpha_i(t))=(t_i^{-1}\bar g(t_it),\bar\alpha(t_it))$. By \eqref{eq:base.length.backward.bounds} we choose base arclength $\theta\in\mathbb{R}/\ell_i\mathbb{Z}$ and normalize the fiber coordinate so that
\[
g_i(1)=d\theta^2+G_i(\theta)(dv+A_i(\theta)d\theta)^2,
\qquad \alpha_i(1)=a_i(\theta)d\theta,
\qquad G_i(0)=1,\qquad c\le\ell_i\le C.
\]
These base identifications and fiber normalizations are fixed in time; in the following time-one estimates, we write $G_i=G_i(1)$ and $a_i=a_i(1)$.
We have
\begin{equation}\label{scalar.in.terms.of.Gi}
    R_i:=R(g_i(1))=
-(\log G_i)''-\frac12((\log G_i)')^2.
\end{equation}
By~\eqref{type.3.estimates.blow.down}, $|R(g_i(1))|\le C$. Evaluating \eqref{scalar.in.terms.of.Gi} at minimum and maximum of the periodic function $(\log G_i)'$ and integrating from $\theta=0$ gives
\[
| (\log G_i)' |\le2\sqrt C,
\qquad C_1^{-1}\le G_i\le C_1.
\]
The smoothing estimates in~\cite{guo2025alpha}, used in Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, give bounds on all covariant derivatives of $R_i$ and $\alpha_i$ on bounded lifted balls. Locally choose the fiber coordinate so that $A_i=0$. Since $\theta$ is base arclength, $\partial_\theta$ is then a unit vector field with $\nabla_{\partial_\theta}\partial_\theta=0$. By \eqref{eq:base.length.backward.bounds}, we have
\[
|\partial_\theta^k R_i|\le C_k,\qquad
|a_i|=|\alpha_i|\le\sqrt2,\qquad
\partial_\theta^k a_i
=(\nabla^k\alpha_i)(\partial_\theta,\ldots,\partial_\theta),
\qquad |\partial_\theta^k a_i|\le C_k.
\]
The identity
\[
G_i''=\frac{(G_i')^2}{2G_i}-R_iG_i
\]
then bounds every derivative of $G_i$ inductively. After parametrizing the bases with a common period $\ell$, the base coefficient at time one is $u_i(1)=(\ell_i/\ell)^2$. Together with these time-one bounds, the metric distortion bounds from~\eqref{type.3.estimates.blow.down}, equations~\eqref{e:invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}, and the positive-time smoothing estimates in~\cite{guo2025alpha} give, after passing to a diagonal subsequence with $\ell_i\to\ell_\infty>0$,
\[
(G_i(t),u_i(t),a_i(t))\longrightarrow(G_\infty(t),u_\infty(t),a_\infty(t))
\quad\text{in }C^\infty(S^1\times[\delta,T]),
\qquad 0<\delta<T<\infty.
\]

By Corollary~\ref{1d.alpha.expander}, we can choose coordinates so that $G_\infty(t)=1$, $u_\infty(t)=t$ and $a_\infty(t)=\sqrt2$. At time one, $(G_i(1),u_i(1),a_i(1))\longrightarrow(1,1,\sqrt2)$ in $C^\infty(S^1)$. Since $\bar\alpha$ evolves by an exact form, and $\alpha_i(1)=\bar\alpha(t_i)$, we can write $\eta_i'=a_i(1)-\sqrt2$ where $\eta_i\in C^\infty(S^1)$ satisfies $\int_0^\ell\eta_i\,d\theta=0$. Then we have $(G_i(1),u_i(1),\eta_i)\longrightarrow(1,1,0)$ in $h^{1+\theta}(S^1)$.
For $s>0$, write the blow-down tuple as
\[
\mathbf{\bar g}_s(t)
=\bigl(s^{-1}G(st),s^{-1}u(st),\eta(st)\bigr),
\qquad a=\sqrt2+\partial_0\eta.
\]
The constant fiber change $S=\sqrt s\,I$ gives
\[
[\mathbf{\bar g}_s(1)]=[(G(s),s^{-1}u(s),\eta(s))].
\] Given $\epsilon>0$, choose $\delta=\delta(\epsilon)>0$ from Corollary~\ref{neighborhood.around.expander}. The preceding convergence implies $[\mathbf{\bar g}_{t_i}(1)]\in\mathcal V_\delta$ for all sufficiently large $i$. Fix $T>0$. Since $t_i\to0$, we have $T/t_i\ge1$ for all sufficiently large $i$. The blow-down by $t_i$, followed by the blow-down by $T/t_i$, is the blow-down by $T$. Therefore Corollary~\ref{neighborhood.around.expander} gives
\[
[\mathbf{\bar g}_T(1)]
=[(\mathbf{\bar g}_{t_i})_{T/t_i}(1)]
\in\mathcal U_\epsilon.
\]
Letting $\epsilon\downarrow0$ gives $[\mathbf{\bar g}_T(1)]=[\mathbf g^*]$ for every $T>0$.
Hence, in the original coordinates,
\[
G'=0,\qquad u(t)=\frac t2a(t)^2.
\]
Substituting into~\eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow} gives
\[
\partial_t G=\partial_t a=\partial_t A=0.
\]
Thus, on the universal cover, $(\bar g(t),\bar\alpha(t))$ is isometric to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{l:collapsing.generators}
    Suppose there exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} (S^1,d)$. Then there are closed curves $\gamma_i,\sigma_i$ such that $[\gamma_i],[\sigma_i]$ generate $\pi_1(M)$ and $L(\gamma_i,g_{s_i}(1))\to0$ and $L(\sigma_i,g_{s_i}(1))\to L(S^1,d)$.
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
By \cite[Theorem 2.6]{cheeger1992nilpotent}, for some sequence $\epsilon_i\to0$, there exists an $\epsilon_i$-almost Riemannian submersion $F_i:(M,g_{s_i}(1))\to (S^1,d)$ such that $\operatorname{diam} F_i^{-1}(\theta)\le \epsilon_i$ for any $\theta\in S^1$.  Let $\gamma_i=F_i^{-1}(\theta)\cong S^1$ be one of the fibers of $F_i$. Then $L(\gamma_i,g_{s_i}(1))\to0$. Let $\sigma_{1,i}$ be the integral curve of the horizontal distribution of $F_i:M\to S^1$ such that $\sigma_{1,i}(0)=x\in M$ and $\sigma_{1,i}(1)\in F_i^{-1}(F_i(x))$. Let $\sigma_{2,i}$ be a shortest curve in $F_i^{-1}(F_i(x))$ connecting $\sigma_{1,i}(1)$ and $x$. Let $\sigma_i=\sigma_{1,i}\cup \sigma_{2,i}$. Then by \cite[Theorem 2.6]{cheeger1992nilpotent}, we have $L(\sigma_i,g_{s_i}(1))\le (1+\epsilon_i)L(S^1,d)+\epsilon_i$.  Since $M=\mathbb{T}^2$ is orientable, $F_i$ is a trivial fibration. Since $F_{i*}[\sigma_i]=[S^1]$, we have $[\gamma_i],[\sigma_i]$ generate $\pi_1(M)$.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{l:zero.pairing.with.fiber}
    Suppose the cohomology class $[\alpha]$ is rational and there exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} (S^1,d)$. Let $\gamma_i,\sigma_i$ be as in Lemma~\ref{l:collapsing.generators}. Then for $i$ sufficiently large, we have $[\alpha](\gamma_i)=0$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
Suppose for contradiction that there exists a sequence of indices $i \to \infty$ such that $[\alpha](\gamma_i)\neq0$. Then Theorem~\ref{growth.of.length.of.shortest.curve} applied to the flow $(g(t),\alpha(t))$ gives $L_{[\gamma_i]}(t)^2\ge\frac{1}{2}[\alpha](\gamma_i)^2 t+L_{[\gamma_i]}(0)^2$. Hence $L_{[\gamma_i]}(s_i)\ge \frac{1}{\sqrt 2}|[\alpha](\gamma_i)|\sqrt {s_i}$. In particular, $L(F_{i}^{-1}(\theta),g_{s_i}(1))\ge s_i^{-1/2}L_{[\gamma_i]}(s_i)\ge \frac{1}{\sqrt 2}|[\alpha](\gamma_i)|>0$. By Lemma~\ref{l:collapsing.generators}, we have $L(F_{i}^{-1}(\theta),g_{s_i}(1))\to0$, forcing $|[\alpha](\gamma_i)| \to 0$. By Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets}, since $[\alpha]$ is rational, if $|[\alpha](\gamma_i)|<\ell([\alpha])$, then $[\alpha](\gamma_i)=0$. This contradicts $[\alpha](\gamma_i)\neq0$.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- theorem-12: body_md -->
\label{no_fiber_component} 
Suppose $[\alpha]$ is rational and there is a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\allowbreak\xrightarrow{GH} (S^1,d)$ and $(\tilde{g}_{s_i}(t), \tilde{\alpha}_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_\infty(t), \tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$. Then $(\tilde g_\infty(t), \tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ has no fiber component.
<!-- end theorem-12 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
Suppose for the sake of contradiction that the limit has a nonzero fiber component. Then, by Corollary~\ref{fiber.constant.in.spacetime}, $\tilde{\alpha}_\infty(\partial_v)(\theta, v, t) = b \neq 0$ for any $(\theta, v, t) \in \mathbb{R}^2 \times (0,\infty)$.

By \cite[Theorem 2.6]{cheeger1992nilpotent}, for $i$ sufficiently large, we have a smooth fibration map $F_i: (M, g_{s_i}(1)) \to (S^1, d)$ which is an $\epsilon_i$-almost Riemannian submersion. Since $M = \mathbb{T}^2$ is orientable and $F_i: M \to S^1$ is a fibration with fiber $S^1$, the fibration $F_i$ must be trivial.

Choose $0<\delta < \operatorname{inj}(S^1,d)$ sufficiently small. By the controlled-contraction argument in the proof of Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, globally trivial short loops contract in the $2\delta$-ball, so the local fundamental group $\pi_1(M,x_i;\delta)$ identifies with restrictions of the deck action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\tilde M$. Let $\gamma_i$ be the fiber of $F_i$ as in Lemma~\ref{l:collapsing.generators} and \ref{l:zero.pairing.with.fiber}. Then, by the same argument as in \cite[Proposition 11.5]{fukaya1990hausdorff}, there exists $n_i \in \mathbb{N}$ such that $0 < \lim_{i\to\infty} d\big([\gamma_i]^{n_i}(p), p\big) \le \delta$ for $p \in \tilde{M}$.

After passing to a subsequence, we may assume that $[\gamma_i]^{n_i} \to \gamma_\delta \in G_\infty$. Consequently, we have $0 < d(\gamma_\delta(p), p) \le \delta$. Since $\delta < \operatorname{inj}(S^1,d)$, $\gamma_\delta$ must be a translation purely in the $\partial_v$ direction.

Normalize and orient $v$ so that the metric on the fiber through $p$ at time one is $dv^2$ and $\gamma_\delta$ translates in the positive direction. Let $h>0$ be the intrinsic distance on this fiber from $p$ to $\gamma_\delta(p)$. Let $\eta_1$ be the curve along the $\mathbb{R}$-orbit from $p$ to $\gamma_\delta(p)$, and let $\eta_2$ be the shortest geodesic with respect to $\tilde{g}_\infty(1)$ from $\gamma_\delta(p)$ to $[\gamma_i]^{n_i}(p)$. Defining $\eta=\eta_1\cup\eta_2$, we have
\begin{align*}
        n_i[\alpha](\gamma_i) &= \int_{\pi(\eta)} \alpha_{s_i} = \int_{\eta} \tilde{\alpha}_{s_i} = \int_{\eta} \tilde{\alpha}_\infty + o(1) \\
        &= \int_{\eta_1} \tilde{\alpha}_\infty + \int_{\eta_2} \tilde{\alpha}_\infty + o(1) \\
        &= bh + \mathcal{E}_i,
    \end{align*}
where the error term $\mathcal{E}_i$ is bounded by
    \[
        |\mathcal{E}_i| \le \sup_{\mathbb{R}^2}|\tilde{\alpha}_\infty|_{\tilde{g}_\infty} d\big(\gamma_\delta(p), [\gamma_i]^{n_i}(p)\big) + o(1).
    \]Since $d\big(\gamma_\delta(p), [\gamma_i]^{n_i}(p)\big) \to 0$ as $i \to \infty$, the error term $\mathcal{E}_i$ vanishes in the limit. Thus, for $i$ sufficiently large, $[\alpha](\gamma_i) \neq 0$. This contradicts Lemma~\ref{l:zero.pairing.with.fiber}.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{l:bounded.generator}
Suppose
\[
(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))
\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))
\quad\text{as }s\to\infty.
\]
Identify $\pi_1(M)$ with the group of deck transformations of the
universal cover and fix a basepoint $0\in\tilde M$. For every $L>0$,
there exist $s_0=s_0(L)>0$ and $L'=\sqrt 8 L>0$ such that for every
$s_1\ge s_0$ and $\gamma\in\pi_1(M)$ satisfying
\[
d_{\tilde g_{s_1}(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)\leq L
\]
we have
\[
\sup_{s\geq s_1}d_{\tilde g_s(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)\leq L'.
\]
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
Let $\varepsilon\in (0,\frac{1}{3})$ and $R>1$, to be chosen below. By smooth convergence, there exists $s_0=s_0(\varepsilon,R)>0$ such that $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))$ are $\varepsilon$-close to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ for any
$s\geq s_0$ on $B_R(g^*(1))\times [R^{-1},R]$. We use the
coordinates $(\theta,v)$ from \eqref{eq:flat_expander_model}.
Write
\[
D_s(\gamma)=d_{\tilde g_s(1)}(\gamma(0),0).
\]For any $\gamma$ satisfying $D_{s_1}(\gamma)\le L$, Theorem \ref{alpha.norm.estimate} gives
\[
|[\alpha](\gamma)|
\le \|\alpha_{s_1}(1)\|_{L^\infty(g_{s_1}(1))}L
\le \sqrt{2}L.
\]

Let $s\geq s_1$ be such that $D_s(\gamma)^2<(1-\varepsilon)R^2$.  We  
write $\gamma(0)=(\theta_0,v_0)$. Then $(v_0^2+\theta_0^2)\le (1-\varepsilon)^{-1}D_s(\gamma)^2< R^2$ and thus $(\theta_0,v_0)\in B_R(g^*(1))$.
Metric comparison
at times $1$ and $1\le r\le R$ gives
\begin{align}
d_{\tilde g_s(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)^2
&\geq (1-\varepsilon)(v_0^2+\theta_0^2),
\label{eq:estimate.g_s(1)}\\
d_{\tilde g_s(r)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)^2
&\leq (1+\varepsilon)(v_0^2+r\theta_0^2).
\label{eq:estimate.g_s(R)}
\end{align}

Because $\tilde\alpha_s(t)$ and $\alpha^*$ are closed, their integrals
depend only on the homotopy class. Integrating along the straight segment
from $0$ to $\gamma(0)$ therefore yields
\[
\left|[\alpha](\gamma)-\sqrt{2}\,\theta_0\right|
\leq \varepsilon\sqrt{v_0^2+\theta_0^2},
\]
where $[\alpha](\gamma)=\int_\gamma\tilde\alpha_s(t)$ is independent of
$s$ and $t$. Consequently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:theta}
\theta_0^2
\leq [\alpha](\gamma)^2
+\varepsilon(v_0^2+\theta_0^2)\le 2L^2+\varepsilon(v_0^2+\theta_0^2).
\end{equation}
The scaling identity $\tilde g_{sR}(1)=R^{-1}\tilde g_s(R)$, together
with \eqref{eq:estimate.g_s(1)}--\eqref{eq:theta}, gives
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dg_sR(1)}
\begin{aligned}
    d_{\tilde g_{sR}(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)^2
\le& (1+\varepsilon)(\frac{v_0^2}{R}+\theta_0^2)\\
\le&(1+\varepsilon) (\frac{d_{\tilde g_s(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)^2}{(1-\varepsilon)R}+2L^2+\varepsilon(v_0^2+\theta_0^2))\\
\le&(1+\varepsilon)2L^2+\frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon} (R^{-1}+\varepsilon)d_{\tilde g_s(1)}\bigl(\gamma(0),0\bigr)^2\\
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Choosing $\varepsilon<\frac{1}{8}$ and $R^2=R(L)^2>\max \{64,(1-\varepsilon)^{-1}8L^2\}$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
D_{sR}(\gamma)^2
\leq 4L^2
+\frac12D_s(\gamma)^2.
\end{equation}
 and thus since $8L^2\le (1-\varepsilon)R^2$, we have
\begin{equation}\label{eq:consequence.of.iteration}
    D_s(\gamma)^2\le 8L^2\Longrightarrow D_{sR}(\gamma)^2\le 8L^2.
\end{equation}

 By \eqref{eq:estimate.g_s(1)} and \eqref{eq:estimate.g_s(R)}, we have for $1\le r\le R$
\[
D_{s_1r}(\gamma)^2\le (1+\varepsilon)(\frac{v_0^2}{r}+\theta_0^2)\le (1+\varepsilon)(v_0^2+\theta_0^2)\le \frac{1+\varepsilon}{1-\varepsilon}D_{s_1}(\gamma)^2\le 2L^2.
\]
Thus $\sup_{s\in [s_1,s_1R]} D_{s}(\gamma)^2\le 2L^2<8L^2$. Applying \eqref{eq:consequence.of.iteration} iteratively gives for any $k\in\mathbb{N}$
\[\sup_{s\in [s_1R^k,s_1R^{k+1}]}D_{s}(\gamma)^2\le 8L^2,\] that is
\[\sup_{s\ge s_1}D_{s}(\gamma)^2\le 8L^2.\]This proves the claim.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
<!-- theorem-13: body_md -->
\label{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality}
Let $s_i\to\infty$ be a sequence such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(X,d)$, and suppose that, on the universal cover,
\[
(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t))
\xrightarrow{C^\infty}
\bigl(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t)\bigr).
\]
Then the following hold:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
    \item If $(X,d)=\{\mathrm{pt}\}$, then $[\alpha]$ is irrational.
    \item If $[\alpha]$ is rational, then $(X,d)=(S^1,d([\alpha]))$.
    \item If, in addition, $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$ with no fiber component, then $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$ implies that $[\alpha]$ is rational. Consequently, by (ii), $(X,d)=(S^1,d([\alpha]))$.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end theorem-13 -->
<!-- proof-28: body_md -->
\textbf{Step 1}: Setup.

As in Example~\ref{example.noncompact invariant expander}, in local coordinates of $\mathbb{R}^2$, we have $g^*(t) = dv^2 + td\theta^2$ and  $\alpha^*(t)=\sqrt 2 d\theta$. We define $u^*=\sqrt 2\theta\in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^2)$ so that $\alpha^* = du^*$. We have the following equivariant convergence:
\begin{center}
    \begin{tikzcd}
  & (\tilde{M},\tilde{g}_{s_i}(1), \tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1), G_{s_i}, p) \arrow[r,  "pt\ eq\ C^\infty"] \arrow[d, "\pi"]
   & (\mathbb{R}^2,g^*(1), \alpha^*(1), G_\infty, p_\infty) \arrow[d,  "\pi_\infty"]\\
  & (M, g_{s_i}(1)) \arrow[r, "GH"]
   & (X,d),
\end{tikzcd}
\end{center}
where $(X, d)$ is either $(S^1, d)$ or a point and $G_\infty = \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$ or $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}$, respectively, by Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}. For each $i$, we write $ g_i:= g_{s_i}(1)$, $\tilde g_i:=\tilde g_{s_i}(1)$, $\alpha_i:=\alpha_{s_i}(1)$ and $\tilde\alpha_i:=\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1)$. Note, for each $i$, there exists $u_i \in C^\infty(\mathbb{R}^2)$, such that $du_{i} = \tilde\alpha_i$. We normalize these functions such that $u_{i}(0) = 0$. By Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets}, we know that $G_i$ acts on the collection of level sets $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$.

\textbf{Step 2}: Passing the action of $G_i$ on level sets to the limit.

Smooth pointed convergence and the normalization $u_i(0)=u^*(0)=0$ give $u_i\to u^*$ in $C^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}$. If $\gamma_i\to\gamma\in G_\infty$, then $[\alpha](\gamma_i)=u_i(\gamma_i(x))-u_i(x)\to u^*(\gamma(x))-u^*(x)$. The limit is independent of $x$, so $G_\infty$ acts on the level sets of $u^*$.

\textbf{Step 3}: We prove (i). If $(X,d)=\{\mathrm{pt}\}$, then $[\alpha]$ is irrational.

By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, $G_\infty=\mathbb{R}^2$ which acts transitively on $\mathbb{R}^2$. Thus, for any $\lambda\in \mathbb{R}$, there is $\gamma\in G_\infty$ such that $u^*(\gamma(x))-u^*(x)=\lambda$. Let $\gamma_i\in G_i$ be such that $\gamma_i\to \gamma$. Then $u_i(\gamma_i(x))-u_i(x)=[\alpha](\gamma_i)$ converges to $\lambda$ as $i\to\infty$. Since the action of $G_i$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is independent of $i$, the action of $G_i$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is not discrete. By Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets}, $[\alpha]$ is irrational. In particular, we know that if $[\alpha]$ is rational, then $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$.

\textbf{Step 4}: Identifying the $\mathbb{R}$ component of $G_\infty$ when $[\alpha]$ is rational.

By Step 3 and Theorem~\ref{no_fiber_component}, $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$ and the limit $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ has no fiber component. By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, $G_\infty \cong \mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$. Since $\alpha^* = \sqrt{2} \, d\theta$, the fiber direction cannot contain a $\partial_\theta$ component, which implies that $\partial_v$ must correspond to the fiber direction. Let $H_\infty = \big\{(\theta,v) \mapsto (\theta,v+v_1) : v_1 \in \mathbb{R} \big\} \le G_\infty$ be the $\mathbb{R}$-subgroup generated by $\partial_v$. We then observe that for any point $x \in \mathbb{R}^2$, if $u^*(x) = r$, the entire orbit $H_\infty \cdot x$ coincides precisely with the level set $(u^*)^{-1}(r)$.

\textbf{Step 5}: We prove (ii). If $[\alpha]$ is rational then $(X,d)=(S^1,d([\alpha]))$.

By Lemmas~\ref{l:collapsing.generators} and \ref{l:zero.pairing.with.fiber}, there exist closed curves $\gamma_i$ and $\sigma_i$ such that $[\gamma_i], [\sigma_i]$ generate $\pi_1(M)$, $L(\gamma_i, g_i) \to 0$, $L(\sigma_i, g_i) \to L(S^1, d)$, and $[\alpha](\gamma_i) = 0$. Since $[\gamma_i], [\sigma_i]$ generate $\pi_1(M)$, Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets} implies that $u_i([\sigma_i](x))-u_i(x)=[\alpha](\sigma_i)=\ell([\alpha])$. By Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets}, the action of $G_i$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in\mathbb{R}\}$ is generated by $r\mapsto r+\ell([\alpha])$ which is independent of $i$. Thus by Step 2, the action of $G_\infty$ on $\{(u^*)^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is a subgroup of $\langle r\mapsto r+\ell([\alpha])\rangle$. 
We now show that the action is the full group. Since $d_{g_i}([\sigma_i](x),x)\le L(\sigma_i,g_i)$ is uniformly bounded, passing to a subsequence yields $[\sigma_i]\to \sigma_\infty\in G_\infty$. Thus we have $u^*(\sigma_\infty(x))-u^*(x)=\ell([\alpha])$. Therefore, the action of $G_\infty$ on $\{(u^*)^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is generated by $ r\mapsto r+\ell([\alpha])$. By Step 4, the orbit $H_\infty \cdot x$ coincides with the level set $(u^*)^{-1}(r)$. Thus $S^1$ has length $\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt 2$.

\textbf{Step 6}: We prove (iii).

    Suppose $L=L(S^1,d)>0$. By Lemma~\ref{l:collapsing.generators}, there exist generators $[\gamma],[\sigma]\in \pi_1(M)$ such that for some $s_1>s_0(2L)$ given in Lemma~\ref{l:bounded.generator}, we have $d_{g_{s_1}(1)}([\gamma](0),0)\le 2L$, $d_{g_{s_1}(1)}([\sigma](0),0)\le 2L$. By Lemma~\ref{l:bounded.generator}, we have $d_{g_s(1)}([\gamma](0),0)\le L'$ and $d_{g_{s}(1)}([\sigma](0),0)\le L'$ for any $s\ge s_1$. Then $G_i\ni[\gamma]\to \gamma_\infty\in G_\infty$ and $G_i\ni[\sigma]\to \sigma_\infty\in G_\infty$. Since $[\gamma],[\sigma]$ are generators, the action of $G_i$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ then fully passes to the limit action of $G_\infty$ on $\{(u^*)^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$. Thus if $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ has no fiber component and $(X,d)=(S^1,d)$, then by the same argument as Step 4, the action of $G_\infty$ on $\{(u^*)^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$ is discrete and so is the action of $G_i$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in \mathbb{R}\}$. Thus $[\alpha]$ is rational by Lemma \ref{action.on.level.sets}.
<!-- end proof-28 -->
<!-- remark-16: body_md -->
Theorem~\ref{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality} is an analog of \cite[Proposition~6.2]{lott2010dimensional}. In the 3D Type III Ricci flow with diameter bounds studied by Lott \cite{lott2010dimensional}, there is a one-to-one correspondence between the expander on the universal cover and the Gromov--Hausdorff limit of the blow-down. In our setting, however, this one-to-one correspondence fails because the same expander $(g^*(t), \alpha^*(t))$ can yield different Gromov--Hausdorff limits. Indeed, as shown in Example~\ref{example.noncompact invariant expander}, $(g^*(t), \alpha^*(t))$ can be quotiented by either an $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$ or an $\mathbb{R} \times \mathbb{R}$ group action to obtain the corresponding base limit. The information determining which quotient is realized is precisely encoded in the cohomology class $[\alpha]$, as manifested in Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets} and the proof above.
<!-- end remark-16 -->
<!-- remark-17: body_md -->
\label{r:S1.not.imply.rational}
       One cannot conclude that $[\alpha]$ is rational merely from the fact that, along a subsequence, the Gromov--Hausdorff limit is $(S^1, d)$ and the limit on the universal cover $(g^*(t+c), \alpha^*(t+c))$ has no fiber component. Indeed, the action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in\mathbb{R}\}$ may be lost in the limit, allowing a sequence of dense actions to converge to a discrete one. For example, in $(\theta,v)$ coordinates, consider the lattices generated by $(\frac{\sqrt 2}{i^2},\frac{1}{i})$ and $(1,0)$. The corresponding action on $\{(u^*)^{-1}(r):r\in\mathbb{R}\}$ is dense for every $i$, but the lattices converge to the group of integer $\theta$-translations and arbitrary $v$-translations, isomorphic to $\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$, which gives a discrete action in the limit. Thanks to Lemma~\ref{l:bounded.generator}, when full-sequence convergence on the universal cover holds, such pathologies cannot occur.
<!-- end remark-17 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{rational.class.alpha.nonzero}
    Suppose $[\alpha]$ is rational. Furthermore, suppose that there exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(\tilde{g}_{s_i}(t),\tilde{\alpha}_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde{\alpha}_\infty(t))$. Then $(\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde{\alpha}_\infty(t))$ cannot be $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$.
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-29: body_md -->
Suppose for contradiction that $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))=(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$. For each $i$, we write $ g_i:= g_{s_i}(1)$, $\tilde g_i:=\tilde g_{s_i}(1)$, $\alpha_i:=\alpha_{s_i}(1)$ and $\tilde\alpha_i:=\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(1)$.
    On $\tilde M$, there exists a function $u_i\in C^\infty(\tilde M)$ such that $\tilde \alpha_i=du_i$. Since $[\alpha]$ is nonzero, the function $u_i:\mathbb{R}^2\to \mathbb{R}$ is surjective. 
    
    Set $\ell=\ell([\alpha])$ and choose $p\in u_i^{-1}(\ell/2)$ and $q\in u_i^{-1}(0)$. By Lemma~\ref{action.on.level.sets}, $u_i([\gamma](q))\in\ell\mathbb{Z}$ for every deck transformation $[\gamma]$. Integrating $du_i=\tilde\alpha_i$ along a curve $c$ from $p$ to $[\gamma](q)$ gives $\ell/2\le L(c)\|\tilde\alpha_i\|_{L^\infty(\tilde g_i)}$. Taking the infimum over these curves and deck transformations yields
    \begin{equation}\label{lower.bound.of.diameter}
        \operatorname{diam}(M,g_i)\ge d_{g_i}(\pi(p),\pi(q))
        \ge \frac{\ell}{2\|\tilde\alpha_i\|_{L^\infty(\tilde g_i)}}.
    \end{equation}
    As $i\to\infty$, smooth pointed convergence to
$(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$ implies that
$|\tilde\alpha_i|_{\tilde g_i}^2\to0$ uniformly on bounded balls.
By Theorem \ref{diameter.bound}, every point of $M$ has a lift
within a fixed distance of the chosen basepoint in $\tilde M$.
Since $\tilde\alpha_i$ and $\tilde g_i$ are deck-invariant,
it follows that
$
\sup_{\tilde M}|\tilde\alpha_i|_{\tilde g_i}^2\to0
$. By \eqref{lower.bound.of.diameter}, this forces $\operatorname{diam}(M, g_i) \to \infty$, directly contradicting the uniform upper bound $\operatorname{diam}(M, g_i) \le C$ from Theorem~\ref{diameter.bound}.
<!-- end proof-29 -->
<!-- remark-18: body_md -->
The argument in Lemma~\ref{rational.class.alpha.nonzero} does not extend to the case where $[\alpha]$ is irrational. In that setting, the action of $\pi_1(M)$ on $\{u_i^{-1}(r):r\in\mathbb{R}\}$ has dense orbits so that there is no contradiction to the diameter bound Theorem~\ref{diameter.bound}.
<!-- end remark-18 -->
<!-- proof-30: body_md -->
Suppose for contradiction that there exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))$ converges in Gromov--Hausdorff topology to a point. Then by Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG} $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_{\infty}(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ where $(\tilde g_{\infty}(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ is $\mathbb{R}^2$-invariant. By Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}, $(\tilde g_{\infty}(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$ is either $(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))$ for some $c\ge 0$ or $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$. Then Lemma~\ref{rational.class.alpha.nonzero} shows that $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))$. We consider a further blow-down $(\tilde g_{\infty,s}(t),\tilde \alpha_{\infty,s}(t))$ and it converges to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$ by Proposition~\ref{p:canonical_alpha_expander_attractor}. For each fixed $r\ge1$, we have $r^{-1}g^*(r+c)=r^{-1}dv^2+(1+c/r)d\theta^2\le g^*(1+c)$, so the diameter of the limiting quotient cannot increase and the base limit remains a point. Equivariant convergence and a diagonal choice as $r\to\infty$ therefore preserve this point limit. Thus there exists $t_i\to\infty$ such that $(\tilde g_{t_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{t_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ and $(M,g_{t_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}$. By Theorem~\ref{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality}, $[\alpha]$ is irrational. This is a contradiction. Therefore, for every sequence $s_i \to \infty$, $(M, g_{s_i}(1))$ converges in the Gromov--Hausdorff sense to $(S^1, d)$ for some metric $d$ on $S^1$. Hence by Proposition~\ref{p:base.S^1.implies.universal.cover.expander} and Theorem~\ref{no_fiber_component}, it follows that $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$. By Theorem~\ref{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality} again, we conclude that $(M,g_{s}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} (S^1,d([\alpha]))$.
<!-- end proof-30 -->
<!-- proof-31: body_md -->
Let $\pi:\mathbb{T}^2\to K$ be the two-sheeted cover of the Klein bottle by the 2-torus $\mathbb{T}^2$. Then $(\pi^*g(t),\pi^*\alpha(t))$ is an $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $\mathbb{T}^2$.

    If $[\alpha]=0$, Lott and Sesum's result~\cite{lott2014ricci} gives $(\mathbb T^2,t^{-1}\pi^*g(t))\allowbreak\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}$ as well as $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))\allowbreak\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$. Since $\operatorname{diam}(K,t^{-1}g(t))\le\operatorname{diam}(\mathbb T^2,t^{-1}\pi^*g(t))$, the Klein bottle also collapses to a point, proving (ii).

    Now suppose that $[\alpha]\neq0$. Then $[\pi^*\alpha]\neq0$, and since $K$ and $\mathbb{T}^2$ share the same universal cover, applying Theorem~\ref{t:main.GH_limit} to $(\pi^*g(t),\pi^*\alpha(t))$ gives $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde\alpha_s(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$.
    
    We now show that the Gromov--Hausdorff limit of $(K,g_s(1))$ is $(S^1,d([\alpha]))$. Let $[\gamma_1],[\gamma_2]$ be the generators of $\pi_1(K)$ such that $[\alpha]([\gamma_1])=\ell([\alpha])$ and $[\alpha]([\gamma_2])=0$. Here we used the fact that $[\gamma_2]$ is torsion in $H_1(K,\mathbb{Z})$ and hence vanishes in $H_1(K,\mathbb{R})$. The generators of $\pi_1(\mathbb{T}^2)$ are $[\gamma_1]^2$, $[\gamma_2]$. We have $[\pi^*\alpha]([\gamma_1]^2)=[\alpha]([\gamma_1]^2)=2\ell([\alpha])$, $[\pi^*\alpha]([\gamma_2])=[\alpha]([\gamma_2])=0$. Then $[\pi^*\alpha]$ is rational and we have $\ell([\pi^*\alpha])=2\ell([\alpha])$. By Theorem~\ref{t:main.GH_limit}, $G_i=\pi_1(\mathbb{T}^2)$ converges to $G_\infty=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{Z}$ which is generated by $\partial_v$ and $\theta\mapsto \theta+ \ell([\pi^*\alpha])/\sqrt {2}$.

    The bounded diameter of the torus cover allows us to choose $\tau_i$ in the left coset $[\gamma_1]G_i$ with uniformly bounded displacement, so a subsequence converges to an isometry $\tau$. The Klein-bottle relation gives $\tau_i[\gamma_2]^n\tau_i^{-1}=[\gamma_2]^{-n}$. By equivariant convergence and discreteness of the period subgroup, every fiber translation $T_h(\theta,v)=(\theta,v+h)$ is a limit of powers of $[\gamma_2]$; hence $\tau T_h\tau^{-1}=T_{-h}$. Since $\tau$ preserves $\alpha^*=\sqrt2\,d\theta$, it has the form $\tau(\theta,v)=(\theta+a,-v+r)$. The periods of $\tau_i$ are odd multiples of $\ell([\alpha])$, so $a$ is an odd multiple of $\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt2$. Composing with elements of $G_\infty$, we may take $a=\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt2$ and $r=0$. Thus the full limiting group is $\mathbb{R}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}$, generated by the fiber translations and this glide, and its quotient is the circle of length $\ell([\alpha])/\sqrt2$. This gives $(K,g_s(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d([\alpha]))$, as claimed.
<!-- end proof-31 -->
<!-- proof-32: body_md -->
By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, after passing to a subsequence if necessary, at least one of the following three alternatives occurs:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\arabic*)]
    \item $(M,g_s(1))\xrightarrow{GH} \{\mathrm{pt}\}$ as $s\to\infty$;

    \item There exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d)$, and on the universal cover $(\tilde{g}_{s_i}(t),\tilde{\alpha}_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde{\alpha}_\infty(t))$ with a nonzero fiber component;

    \item There exists a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d)$, and on the universal cover $(\tilde{g}_{s_i}(t),\tilde{\alpha}_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde{g}_\infty(t),\tilde{\alpha}_\infty(t))$ with no fiber component.
\end{enumerate}
    We will show that in each of the cases above, there exists a sequence $s_i' \to \infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i'}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} \{\mathrm{pt}\}$ and $(\tilde{g}_{s_i'}(t), \tilde{\alpha}_{s_i'}(t)) \xrightarrow{C^\infty} (g^*(t), \alpha^*(t))$.


\noindent\textbf{Case 1}


    Suppose for contradiction that for any sequence  $s_i\to\infty$, $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t))$ stays uniformly bounded away from $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$. By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG} and that $(M,g_s(1))\xrightarrow{GH} \{\mathrm{pt}\}$, there is a convergent subsequence converging to $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ which is $\mathbb{R}^2$-invariant. By Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}, $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ is either $(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))$ for some $c\ge0$ or $(g_{\mathrm {Euc}},0)$. 
    
    If $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))=(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))$, then we consider a further blow-down sequence $(\tilde g_{\infty,s}(t),\tilde \alpha_{\infty,s}(t))$ and it converges to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ as $s\to\infty$ by Proposition~\ref{p:canonical_alpha_expander_attractor}. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{t:main.GH_limit}(i), there exists $t_i\to\infty$ such that $(\tilde g_{t_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{t_i}(t))$ converges to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$. This contradicts our assumption. Thus  $(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde \alpha_\infty(t))=(g_{\mathrm {Euc}},0)$.
    
    By the above argument, for every sequence $s_i \to \infty$, there exists a subsequence such that $(\tilde{g}_{s_i}(t), \tilde{\alpha}_{s_i}(t)) \xrightarrow{C^\infty} (g_{\mathrm{Euc}}, 0)$. Consequently, the full family $(\tilde{g}_s(t), \tilde{\alpha}_s(t))$ converges smoothly to $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}}, 0)$ as $s \to \infty$. In particular, $|R(\tilde{g}_s(t))| \to 0$ and $|\tilde{\alpha}_s(t)|^2_{\tilde{g}_s(t)} \to 0$ in $C^\infty_{\mathrm{loc}}$ as $s \to \infty$. Since $\operatorname{diam}(M, g_s(1)) \to 0$ as $s \to \infty$, it follows that $s |R(g(s))| \to 0$ and $s |\alpha(s)|_{g(s)}^2 \to 0$ uniformly on $M$.
    
    
Therefore, given any $\epsilon > 0$, there exists $T > 0$ such that for all $t \ge T$,
\[
    |R(g(t))| \le \epsilon t^{-1} \quad \text{and} \quad |\alpha(t)|_{g(t)}^2 \le \epsilon t^{-1}.
\]
By Theorem~\ref{growth.of.length.of.shortest.curve}, for any class $\sigma \in H_1(M, \mathbb{Z})$, the length $L_\sigma(t)$ of the shortest geodesic representative satisfies
\[
    D^+ L_\sigma(t) \le \int_0^{L_\sigma(t)} \left( -\frac{1}{2} R + \frac{1}{4} (\alpha(\gamma'))^2 \right) ds \le \epsilon t^{-1} L_\sigma(t).
\]
Integrating this differential inequality from $T$ to $t$, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{L.sigma<=t^epsilon}
    L_\sigma(t) \le \left( \frac{t}{T} \right)^\epsilon L_\sigma(T).
\end{equation}
On the other hand, equation \eqref{growth.L_sigma} in Theorem~\ref{growth.of.length.of.shortest.curve} asserts that
\begin{equation}\label{L.sigma>=t^1/2}
    L_\sigma(t)^2 \ge \frac{1}{2} [\alpha](\sigma)^2 (t-T) + L_\sigma(T)^2.
\end{equation}
Since $[\alpha] \neq 0$, we can choose $\sigma \in H_1(M, \mathbb{Z})$ such that $[\alpha](\sigma) \neq 0$. Choosing $\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}$, \eqref{L.sigma<=t^epsilon} contradicts \eqref{L.sigma>=t^1/2} for $t$ large.

\noindent\textbf{Case 2}

    We know that $(\tilde g_\infty(t), \tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ reduces to an $\mathbb{R}$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow on $S^1$. Writing $(\tilde g_\infty(t), \tilde \alpha_\infty(t))$ as in \eqref{invariant.flow}, the flow satisfies system \eqref{gauged.invariant.alpha.ricci.flow}, which takes the form
    \begin{align}
	\frac{\partial G}{\partial t}=&\Delta_uG- G^{-1}|\nabla_uG|^2+\frac{1}{2}b^2\label{eqn.G}\\
	\frac{\partial A}{\partial t}=&\frac{1}{2}a bG^{-1}\label{eqn.A}\\
	\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=&\frac{1}{2}G^{-2}(G')^2+\frac{1}{2}a^2\label{eqn.u}\\
	\frac{\partial a}{\partial t}=&[u^{-1/2}(au^{-1/2})']'-\frac{1}{2}a b^2G^{-1}\label{eqn.a}.
\end{align}
where $f'=\frac{df}{d\theta}$, $\nabla_u,\Delta_u$ are the gradient and Laplacian with respect to the metric $ud\theta^2$ on $S^1$ and $b=\tilde\alpha_\infty(\partial_v)\neq0$ is a spacetime constant by assumption and Corollary~\ref{fiber.constant.in.spacetime}.

Throughout this argument, we assume $t\ge 1$ and $C$ is some constant depending on time-one data.
Applying the maximum principle on \eqref{eqn.G} we have $\frac{1}{2}b^2t+ m\le G\le \frac{1}{2}b^2t+ M$ where $m=\min_{ S^1}G(1)-\frac{1}{2}b^2$ and $M=\max_{ S^1}G(1)-\frac{1}{2}b^2$. Setting $w=\log G$, we compute
\[\frac{\partial w}{\partial t}=\Delta_uw+\frac{1}{2}b^2G^{-1}.\]
Setting $Z=|\nabla w|^2=u^{-1}w'^2=u^{-1}\frac{G'^2}{G^2}$, we obtain
\[\frac{\partial Z}{\partial t}=\Delta_u Z-2|\nabla^2w|^2-\frac{1}{2}Z^2-\frac{1}{2}u^{-1}a^2Z-b^2G^{-1}Z\le\Delta_u Z-\frac{b^2}{\frac{1}{2}b^2t+M}Z.\]
Then by the maximum principle, we have \[u^{-1}\frac{G'^2}{G^2}\le \max_{S^1} Z(t)\le\max_{S^1} Z(1)\left(\frac{1+2M/b^2}{t+2M/b^2}\right)^2.\]
Let $Q=|ad\theta|^2=a^2/u$. By \eqref{eqn.a} and \eqref{eqn.u}, we have
\[
	\frac{\partial Q}{\partial t}=\Delta_uQ-2|\nabla (ad\theta)|^2- b^2G^{-1}Q-\frac{\partial_t u}{u}Q\le \Delta_u Q-\frac{b^2}{\frac{1}{2}b^2t+M}Q.\]
Applying the maximum principle, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{e:estimate.Q}
    u^{-1}a^2\le \max_{S^1} Q(t)\le\max_{S^1} Q(1)\left(\frac{1+2M/b^2}{t+2M/b^2}\right)^2.
\end{equation}
Thus we have
\[\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{2}G^{-2}(G')^2+\frac{1}{2}a^2\le \frac{Cu}{(t+2M/b^2)^2}.\]
Solving the differential inequality, we have \[\max_{S^1} u(t)\le \max_{S^1}u(1)\exp [C(\frac{1}{1+2M/b^2}-\frac{1}{t+2M/b^2})].\]
Plugging this into \eqref{e:estimate.Q} we have $|a(t)|\le C (t+2M/b^2)^{-1}$. Putting the estimates for $a$ and $G$ back to \eqref{eqn.A}, we have
\[|\frac{\partial A}{\partial t}|\le \frac{C}{(t+2M/b^2)^2}.\]
This gives
\[|A(t)|\le |A(1)|+C(\frac{1}{1+2M/b^2}-\frac{1}{t+2M/b^2}).\] 

To summarize, we have 
\begin{equation}\label{estimates.component.fiber.component}
    G(t)\sim \frac{1}{2}b^2t,\quad A(t)=O(1),\quad u(t)=O(1),\quad a(t)=O(\frac{1
}{t}).
\end{equation}
Since the fibers are $G_\infty$-orbits, the quotient metric at time $t$
is $u(\theta,t)\,d\theta^2$.
For each fixed $T>0$, equivariant convergence gives $(M,(s_iT)^{-1}g(s_iT))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,T^{-1}u(\theta,T)\,d\theta^2)$ as $i\to\infty$.  By
\eqref{estimates.component.fiber.component}, $\operatorname{diam}(S^1,T^{-1}u(\theta,T)\,d\theta^2)\to0$ as $T\to\infty$. By a diagonal choice, select $T_j\to\infty$ and increasing indices $i_j$ such that $s_j'=s_{i_j}T_j$ satisfies $\operatorname{diam}(M,g_{s_j'}(1))\to0$ and
\[
\left|
|\tilde\alpha_{s_j'}(1)|_{\tilde g_{s_j'}(1)}^2(\tilde p)
-T_j|\tilde\alpha_\infty(T_j)|_{\tilde g_\infty(T_j)}^2(\tilde p)
\right|\longrightarrow0.
\]
Since the component estimates and the positive lower bound for $u$ give
\[
t|\tilde\alpha_\infty(t)|_{\tilde g_\infty(t)}^2
=
t\left(\frac{a(\theta,t)^2}{u(\theta,t)}
+\frac{b^2}{G(\theta,t)}\right)
\longrightarrow2
\]
uniformly in $\theta$, this diagonal choice also ensures $|\tilde\alpha_{s_j'}(1)|_{\tilde g_{s_j'}(1)}^2(\tilde p)\to2$.

By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG}, after passing to a subsequence,
the flows on the universal cover converge to an
$\mathbb R^2$-invariant solution. By
Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}, the limit is either
$(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$ or $(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*)$ for some $c\ge0$.
The time-one norm limit excludes the former and, since
$
|\alpha^*|_{g^*(1+c)}^2=\frac{2}{1+c},
$
forces $c=0$ in the latter. Thus $(\tilde g_{s_j'}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_j'}(t))
\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$, while $(M,g_{s_j'}(1))\xrightarrow{GH} \{\mathrm{pt}\}$.

\noindent\textbf{Case 3}

If there is a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ satisfying Case 2, then we are done.

Otherwise, we may assume that whenever $s_i\to\infty$ satisfies $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(S^1,d)$ and $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))$, the limit has no fiber component. We are going to deduce a contradiction.

We claim that there exists $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}$. Suppose for contradiction that any $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}(X,d)$, we have $X=S^1$. By Corollary \ref{c:convergence_CFG}, for any $s_i\to\infty$, there is a subsequence such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))$ converges. Then by our assumption and Proposition~\ref{p:base.S^1.implies.universal.cover.expander} $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ with no fiber component. Then every subsequential limit on the universal cover is $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$, so we get the full sequence convergence $(\tilde g_{s}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ with no fiber component. By Theorem~\ref{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality}(iii), $[\alpha]$ is rational, contradicting our assumption that $[\alpha]$ is irrational.

Thus we get a sequence $s_i\to\infty$ such that $(M,g_{s_i}(1))\xrightarrow{GH}\{\mathrm{pt}\}$. By Corollary~\ref{c:convergence_CFG} and Lemma~\ref{l:R2.invariant.solution}, after passing to a subsequence, 
\[
(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde\alpha_{s_i}(t))\xrightarrow{C^\infty}(\tilde g_\infty(t),\tilde\alpha_\infty(t))
=(g^*(t+c),\alpha^*(t+c))\quad\text{or}\quad(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0).
\]
In the first case, Proposition~\ref{p:canonical_alpha_expander_attractor} and a further blow-down give the desired sequence for Theorem \ref{t:main.GH_limit}(ii), with the Gromov-Hausdorff limit being a point as argued in the proof of Theorem~\ref{t:main.GH_limit}(i). It remains to rule out the possibility that every sequence whose Gromov--Hausdorff limit is a point has $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$ as its limit on the universal cover.

Under this assumption, the only possible subsequential blow-down limits on the universal cover are $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ and $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$: the first occurs when the Gromov--Hausdorff limit downstairs is a circle, and the second when it is a point. We claim that these two limits cannot both occur. Indeed, by continuity of $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))$ in $s$, if both occurred, one could choose a sequence of $s_i\to\infty$ for which $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))$ stays  a definite distance away from both limits. By Corollary \ref{c:convergence_CFG}, there is a convergent subsequence $(\tilde g_{s_i}(t),\tilde \alpha_{s_i}(t))$ whose limit is neither $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ nor $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$. This is a contradiction. Thus $(\tilde g_s(t),\tilde \alpha_s(t))$ converges to one of $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$ or $(g_{\mathrm{Euc}},0)$. Since there is already a subsequence whose Gromov-Hausdorff limit is a circle, the full family must converge to $(g^*(t),\alpha^*(t))$. Now Theorem~\ref{t:GH_limit_vs_rationality}(iii) again implies that $[\alpha]$ is rational, a contradiction.
<!-- end proof-32 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{linearization}
The linearization of $Q(\mathbf{\hat g})$ at $\mathbf g=(G_*,1,c)$, where $c$ is a constant, is given by
\[
DQ(\mathbf {g})(h,v,\xi)=L_{\mathbf g}(h,v,\xi)
=(h'',\,3v''-v+\sqrt2\xi',\,\xi''+\sqrt2v').
\]
Its kernel is $\{(H,0,c):H^T=H\text{ is constant},\ c\text{ is constant}\}$.
Zero is a semisimple eigenvalue and all other eigenvalues have strictly negative real part.
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-33: body_md -->
At the fixed point, $X=0$ and $\delta X^0=\frac32v'$. Direct differentiation gives the displayed operator. With
\[
\langle(h_1,v_1,\xi_1),(h_2,v_2,\xi_2)\rangle
=\int_0^\ell\left(\operatorname{tr}(G_*^{-1}h_1G_*^{-1}h_2)+v_1v_2+\xi_1\xi_2\right)dx^0,
\]
the diagonal part is self-adjoint and the first-order coupling is anti-self-adjoint. Therefore
\[
\langle L_{\mathbf g}(h,v,\xi),(h,v,\xi)\rangle
=-\|h'\|^2-3\|v'\|^2-\|v\|^2-\|\xi'\|^2.
\]
This gives the kernel. The subspace where $h$ and $\xi$ have zero mean, with $v$ arbitrary, is invariant, and the Poincar\'e inequality gives a strictly negative bound there, excluding generalized zero eigenvectors on this subspace. Its invariant complement consists of $(H,0,c)$ with $H$ and $c$ constant, on which $L_{\mathbf g}=0$. Thus zero is a semisimple eigenvalue. Ellipticity on the circle gives compact resolvent and a spectral gap.
<!-- end proof-33 -->
<!-- definition-11: body_md -->
With $h^r$ and $\mathbb V$ as in Definition~\ref{tuple.equivalence}, set
\[
\mathbb X_0=h^\rho(S^1;\mathbb V),\qquad
\mathbb X_1=h^{2+\rho}(S^1;\mathbb V).
\]
For $\frac12<\beta<1-\frac\rho2$, continuous interpolation gives
$\mathbb X_\beta=(\mathbb X_0,\mathbb X_1)_\beta
=h^{1+\theta}(S^1;\mathbb V)$, where $1+\theta=\rho+2\beta$ and $\rho<\theta<1$.
<!-- end definition-11 -->
<!-- theorem-14: body_md -->
\label{stability}
Fix $\mathbf g=(G_*,1,0)$ and $0<\rho<\theta<1$ as above. There is a neighborhood $\mathcal U$ of $\mathbf g$ in $h^{1+\theta}(S^1;\mathbb V)$ such that every initial datum $\mathbf{\tilde g}(0)\in\mathcal U$ gives a global solution of~\eqref{rescaled.equation}. The corresponding solution of~\eqref{rescaled.gauged.equation} converges exponentially in $C^{2+\rho}$, for $\tau\ge1$, to an equilibrium
\[
\mathbf g_\infty=(G_\infty,1,c_\infty),\qquad G_\infty\text{ constant positive definite}.
\]
In the original normalized coordinates, $\mathbf{\tilde g}(\tau)$ converges exponentially in $C^{1+\rho}$ to
\[
\bigl(G_\infty,(\varphi_\infty')^2,
 c_\infty+\sqrt2(\varphi_\infty-x^0)\bigr),
\]
where $\varphi_\infty$ is an orientation-preserving $C^{2+\rho}$ base diffeomorphism. Moreover, $\tilde\alpha=(\sqrt2+~\tilde\eta')dx^0$ converges exponentially in $C^{1+\rho}$ for $\tau\ge1$ to $\sqrt2\,d\varphi_\infty$.
If the initial data are smooth, these convergences hold in every $C^k$ norm, with constants depending on higher initial norms.
<!-- end theorem-14 -->
<!-- proof-34: body_md -->
Our proof mainly follows the strategy of \cite[Theorem 3]{knopf2009convergence}.  Fix $0<\sigma<\rho$ and set $\mathbb E_0=h^\sigma(S^1;\mathbb V)$ and $\mathbb E_1=h^{2+\sigma}(S^1;\mathbb V)$. Then $\mathbb X_1\subset\mathbb E_1\subset\mathbb X_0\subset\mathbb E_0$ continuously and densely. For $\frac12<\beta<1-\frac\rho2$, both $\mathbb X_\beta$ and $\mathbb E_\beta=(\mathbb E_0,\mathbb E_1)_\beta$ embed into $C^1$.

\noindent\textbf{Step 1.} By Proposition~\ref{linearization}, $L_{\mathbf g}=DQ(\mathbf g)=L_{\mathrm{sa}}+F$, where $L_{\mathrm{sa}}(h,v,\xi)=(h'',3v''-v,\xi'')$ and $F(h,v,\xi)=(0,\sqrt2\xi',\sqrt2v')$. The complexification $L_{\mathrm{sa}}^{\mathbb C}$ is strictly elliptic and self-adjoint with non-positive spectrum. By Schauder estimates, it is sectorial (see \cite[Definition 1]{knopf2009convergence}). Since $F^{\mathbb C}$ is bounded from $\mathbb X_\beta$ to $\mathbb X_0$ and from $\mathbb E_\beta$ to $\mathbb E_0$, \cite[Proposition 2.4.1]{lunardi2012analytic} implies that $L_{\mathbf g}^{\mathbb C}$ is sectorial on $\mathbb X_0$ and $\mathbb E_0$, with domains $\mathbb X_1$ and $\mathbb E_1$, respectively.

The principal part of $Q$ is diagonal with coefficients $u^{-1},3,u^{-1}$; the remaining terms depend smoothly on the variables and their first derivatives. Near $\mathbf g$, positivity and uniform parabolicity hold. With $\nu=(\rho-\sigma)/2$, we have $\mathbb X_0=(\mathbb E_0,\mathbb E_1)_\nu$ and $\mathbb X_\beta=(\mathbb E_0,\mathbb E_1)_{\beta+\nu}$, where $\beta+\nu<1$. Thus interpolation, the little-H\"older product estimates, and Schauder graph-norm equivalence verify the hypotheses of Simonett's theorem as in \cite[Lemmas 1 and 2]{knopf2009convergence}.

By Proposition~\ref{linearization}, zero is a semisimple eigenvalue and the remaining spectrum lies in the set $\{z:\operatorname{Re}z\le-\omega_0\}$ for some $\omega_0>0$. Simonett's theorem \cite{simonett1995center}, in the form of \cite[Theorem 4]{knopf2009convergence}, gives the spectral decomposition $\mathbb X_a=\mathbb X_a^{\mathrm s}\oplus\mathbb X_a^{\mathrm c}$ for $a\in[0,1]$, where $\mathbb X_a^{\mathrm c}=\ker L_{\mathbf g}$ has dimension $N(N+1)/2+1$. Write $\pi^{\mathrm s}:\mathbb X_a\to \mathbb X_a^{\mathrm s},\pi^{\mathrm c}:\mathbb X_a\to \mathbb X_a^{\mathrm c}$ for the corresponding projections. For each $r\in\mathbb N$, there is a locally invariant $C^r$ center manifold $\Gamma_{\mathrm{loc}}^r=\{z+\gamma_d^r(z):z\in B(\mathbb X_1^{\mathrm c},\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf g,d)\}$, with $\gamma_d^r:\mathbb X_a^{\mathrm c}\to \mathbb X_a^{\mathrm s}$ satisfying $\gamma_d^r(\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf g)=\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf g$ and $D\gamma_d^r(\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf g)=0$. For $0<\omega<\omega_0$, after decreasing $d$, its attraction estimate is
\begin{equation}\label{exponential.converge.to.center.manifold}
\begin{aligned}
&\|\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\gamma_d^r(\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau))\|_{\mathbb X_1}\\
&\qquad\le C\tau^{\beta-1}e^{-\omega\tau}
\|\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf{\hat g}(0)-\gamma_d^r(\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf{\hat g}(0))\|_{\mathbb X_\beta},
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
for $\tau>0$ as long as the solution remains in $B(\mathbb X_\beta,\mathbf g,d)$.

\noindent\textbf{Step 2.} We show that the center manifold coincides locally with the moduli space $\mathcal M(\mathbf g,d)$ of fixed points. If $\mathbf g'$ is a sufficiently close fixed point, its stationary trajectory and~\eqref{exponential.converge.to.center.manifold} imply $\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf g'=\gamma_d^r(\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf g')$. Thus $\mathbf g'\in\Gamma_{\mathrm{loc}}^r$.

Conversely, every tuple $(H,1,c)$, with $H$ constant positive definite and $c\in\mathbb{R}$, is a fixed point. Its projection onto $\ker L_{\mathbf g}$ parametrizes a neighborhood of $\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf g$. By the preceding inclusion, these fixed points fill the center-manifold graph, so $\gamma_d^r\equiv\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf g$. Thus, after shrinking the neighborhood, $\mathcal M(\mathbf g,d)=\Gamma_{\mathrm{loc}}^r=:\Gamma$, independently of $r$. Denote its graphing function by $\gamma$.

\noindent\textbf{Step 3.} By \cite[(2.2)]{knopf2009convergence}, $\mathbb X_\beta=h^{1+\theta}$ with $1+\theta=\rho+2\beta$. Set $d_0=\|\mathbf{\tilde g}(0)-\mathbf g\|_{\mathbb X_\beta}$ and fix $0<\lambda<\omega$ and $0<\epsilon<\delta<d$. For $d_0<\epsilon$ sufficiently small, local well-posedness and smooth dependence, together with positive-time smoothing \cite[Theorem 3.1 and Remark 3.3]{simonett1995center}, give $\sup_{0\le\tau\le1}\|\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\mathbf g\|_{\mathbb X_\beta}+\|\mathbf{\hat g}(1)-\mathbf g\|_{\mathbb X_1}\le C_0d_0$.

For $\tau\ge1$, write $z(\tau)=\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)$. Since $Q(z(\tau)+\gamma(z(\tau)))=0$, local Lipschitz continuity of $Q:\mathbb X_1\to\mathbb X_0$, equivalence of norms on the center space, and~\eqref{exponential.converge.to.center.manifold} give
\begin{equation}\label{exponential.decay.time.derivative}
\begin{aligned}
\|\partial_\tau z(\tau)\|_{\mathbb X_1}
&=\|\pi^{\mathrm c}\partial_\tau\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)\|_{\mathbb X_1}\\
&=\|\pi^{\mathrm c}Q(\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau))\|_{\mathbb X_1}\\
&=\left\|\pi^{\mathrm c}\bigl[Q(\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau))
-Q(z(\tau)+\gamma(z(\tau)))\bigr]\right\|_{\mathbb X_1}\\
&\le C\|\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\gamma(z(\tau))\|_{\mathbb X_1}\\
&\le Ce^{-\lambda\tau}d_0.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Choosing $\epsilon\ll\delta$, integrating this estimate from time one, and combining it with \eqref{exponential.converge.to.center.manifold}, we obtain
\begin{equation}\label{eq:initial.space.stability}
\|\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\mathbf g\|_{\mathbb X_\beta}
\le C d_0(1+\lambda^{-1})<\delta<d
\end{equation}
on every interval on which the solution remains in $B(\mathbb X_\beta,\mathbf g,d)$. This bound, together with local well-posedness \cite[Theorem 3.1]{simonett1995center} and positive-time parabolic estimates, gives existence for all $\tau\ge0$. Integrating~\eqref{exponential.decay.time.derivative} from $\tau$ to infinity yields a limit $z_\infty$ in the center space. Combining its exponential convergence in $\mathbb X_1$ with \eqref{exponential.converge.to.center.manifold} gives
\[
\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)
=\pi^{\mathrm c}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)+\pi^{\mathrm s}\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)
\longrightarrow z_\infty+\gamma(z_\infty)
=:\mathbf g_\infty=(G_\infty,1,c_\infty)\in\Gamma
\]
exponentially in $\mathbb X_1$ for $\tau\ge1$. Parabolic Schauder estimates then give $\|\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\mathbf g_\infty\|_{C^k}\le C_ke^{-\lambda_k\tau}d_0$ for every $k$ and $\tau\ge1$.

To return to the original coordinates, choose $\rho<\rho_1<\theta$. Parabolic smoothing and the definition of $X$ give
\[
\|X(\tau)\|_{C^{2+\rho_1}}
\le Cd_0\begin{cases}
\tau^{-1+(\theta-\rho_1)/2},&0<\tau\le1,\\
e^{-\lambda\tau},&\tau\ge1.
\end{cases}
\]
The short-time bound is integrable. Standard estimates, as in \cite[Lemma 3.5]{guenther2002stability}, therefore give an orientation-preserving $C^{2+\rho}$ diffeomorphism $\varphi_\infty$ with $\|\varphi_\tau-\varphi_\infty\|_{C^{2+\rho}}\le Ce^{-\lambda\tau}d_0$ for $\tau\ge1$. The pullback formulas~\eqref{eq:base.pullback.stability} yield the asserted exponential $C^{1+\rho}$ convergence of $\mathbf{\tilde g}$. Separately, $\tilde\alpha=\varphi_\tau^*((\sqrt2+\partial_0\hat\eta)dx^0)$ converges exponentially in $C^{1+\rho}$ to $\sqrt2\,d\varphi_\infty$, using the $C^{2+\rho}$ convergence of $\hat\eta$ and $\varphi_\tau$. For smooth initial data, $X$ is smooth up to $\tau=0$, and the same argument applies in every $C^k$ norm, with constants depending on higher initial norms.
<!-- end proof-34 -->
<!-- corollary-7: body_md -->
\label{neighborhood.around.expander}
For every $\epsilon>0$ there exists $\delta=\delta(\epsilon)>0$ such that a smooth locally $\mathbb{R}^N$-invariant $\alpha$-Ricci flow $(\bar g(t),\bar \alpha(t))$ with trivial linear holonomy, no fiber component, and $\int_{S^1}\bar\alpha=\sqrt2\ell$ satisfies
\[
[\mathbf{\bar g}(1)]\in\mathcal V_\delta
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
[\mathbf{\bar g}_s(1)]\in\mathcal U_\epsilon
\qquad\text{for all }s\ge1.
\]
Here $\mathbf{\bar g}(t)=(G(t),u(t),\eta(t))$, and $\mathbf{\bar g}_s(1)=(s^{-1}G(s),s^{-1}u(s),\eta(s))$.
<!-- end corollary-7 -->
<!-- proof-35: body_md -->
By~\eqref{eq:distance.to.expander.class}, choose a representative $\mathbf{\tilde g}_0$ of $[\mathbf{\bar g}(1)]$ such that
\[
d_0=\|\mathbf{\tilde g}_0-\mathbf g^*\|_{h^{1+\theta}(S^1;\mathbb V)}<\delta.
\]
Let $\mathbf{\tilde g}(\tau)$ and $\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)$ solve~\eqref{rescaled.equation} and~\eqref{rescaled.gauged.equation}, respectively, with initial tuple $\mathbf{\tilde g}_0$ at logarithmic time $\tau=0$, corresponding to physical time $t=1$. By Theorem~\ref{stability},~\eqref{eq:initial.space.stability}, and~\eqref{eq:base.pullback.stability},
\[
\begin{aligned}
 d_{1+\rho}([\mathbf{\tilde g}(\tau)],[\mathbf g^*])
 &=d_{1+\rho}([\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)],[\mathbf g^*])\\
 &\le\|\mathbf{\hat g}(\tau)-\mathbf g^*\|_{h^{1+\rho}}
 \le Cd_0<C\delta,\qquad \tau\ge0.
\end{aligned}
\]
The equality follows from the base pullback~\eqref{eq:base.pullback.stability}. The last bound follows from~\eqref{eq:initial.space.stability} and the inclusion $h^{1+\theta}\subset h^{1+\rho}$. Equation~\eqref{rescaled.equation} is invariant under the transformations defining the tuple classes, so $[\mathbf{\tilde g}(\tau)]=[(G(e^\tau),e^{-\tau}u(e^\tau),\eta(e^\tau))]$. For $s\ge1$, the constant fiber change $S=\sqrt s\,I$ gives
\[
[\mathbf{\bar g}_s(1)]
=[(G(s),s^{-1}u(s),\eta(s))]
=[\mathbf{\tilde g}(\log s)].
\]
Choose $\delta$ sufficiently small that Theorem~\ref{stability} applies and $C\delta<\epsilon$. The preceding estimate at $\tau=\log s$ then gives $[\mathbf{\bar g}_s(1)]\in\mathcal U_\epsilon$ for every $s\ge1$.
<!-- end proof-35 -->
